stands / then one throws onto this metal as much **double salt** <note>original: "Salis duplicati," likely potassium sulfate.</note> as will be necessary to cover it / namely half a finger thick / and when it is well-melted like **fluid** <term>fluid</term> <note>original: ▽, the alchemical symbol for water or fluidity.</note> / one stirs it often with an iron rod / which one repeats / until it is well **calcined** <term>calcination</term> <note>The process of heating a substance to high temperatures in air to reduce it to a powder or "ash."</note> / and the **lead** becomes like a thick porridge / during which one always throws new **salt** <term>salt</term> <note>original: ⊖, the alchemical symbol for salt.</note> onto it / namely as soon as the previous batch is consumed; afterwards one takes away all the thick porridge that has settled on top of the **lead** <term>lead</term> <note>original: ♄, the alchemical symbol for Saturn/lead.</note> with a special iron / which will not yet be perfectly calcined.

Then one throws new **double salt** <note>original: "⊖ duplicatum"</note> onto the **body** <note>original: "Corpus," referring to the bulk material.</note> of **lead** <term>lead</term> <note>original: ♄.</note> again / as happened before / and as often as is necessary / until this body is entirely calcined / and brought to **ashes**. After these ashes have become quite cold / one pounds them small / and afterwards sifts them through a fine sieve / and thus one will obtain a **yellow powder** from it / then one melts and calcines anew / as mentioned above / that which remained behind in the sieve / and repeats this operation until everything has been brought into a very fine powder.

This powder one calcines often with yellow **sulfur** <term>sulfur</term> <note>original: 🜍, the alchemical symbol for sulfur.</note> / and **fixes** <term>fixation</term> <note>To "fix" a substance meant to make it stable in heat so it would not evaporate or fly away.</note> it with **lime** / which one afterwards separates from it again by washing / this fixed powder one puts into a large glass / with an equal amount of **mineral marcasite** <note>original: "Marcasitæ mineralis," a term then used for various shiny metallic minerals like pyrite.</note> / which has been in the fire / which one makes into a fine powder after its roasting: Then

then one pours onto this mass a strong **salt of antimony** <term>antimony salt</term> <note>original: ☌ 🜔; ☌ is the symbol for antimony, a brittle metallic element, and 🜔 is the symbol for salt.</note>, from which all **phlegm** <term>phlegm</term> <note>In alchemy, the "phlegm" is the watery, tasteless, and medically inactive part of a substance produced during distillation.</note> has been separated / and which is made from such a **salt** <term>salt</term> <note>original: 🜔</note> that is of a **copper-like** <note>original: "♀nerischer," referring to the planet and goddess Venus, associated with the metal copper.</note> quality.

After the final distillation, one places that which remained in the glass onto molten **lead** / and **cupels** <term>cupellation</term> <note>A refining process where ores or alloyed metals are treated under high heat to separate noble metals, like gold and silver, from base metals like lead.</note> it with 2 third-parts of fresh **lead** <term>lead</term> <note>original: ♄, the symbol for Saturn/lead.</note> / and thus this metal / after it has been purified by means of **mercury** <term>mercury</term> <note>original: ☿</note> **fixed** <note>In alchemy, "fixing" a substance means making a volatile substance stable so it does not evaporate when heated.</note> with **quicklime** / its elements secured / and having been boiled through the **oil of salt** <term>oil of salt</term> <note>original: "Oleum 🜔"; likely a concentrated form of hydrochloric acid.</note> / will be transformed into good **silver** / which holds no small amount of **gold** hidden within itself.

If one wishes to use **aqua fortis** <term>aqua fortis</term> <note>Latin for "strong water"; nitric acid, used to separate silver from gold.</note> for the separation of this **gold** <term>gold</term> <note>original: ☉, the symbol for the Sun/gold.</note> / which sometimes makes up nearly half of this composition / then one must necessarily add to it twice its weight in good **silver** / then the **aqua fortis** will attack the **silver** <term>silver</term> <note>original: ☽, the symbol for the Moon/silver.</note> / and let the **gold** fall / turning it into a black powder / which one **sweetens** <term>sweetening</term> <note>The process of washing a precipitate with water to remove any remaining acids or salts.</note> / dries / and melts with **borax** into a metallic body.

### No. 12.

### How Mercury <note>original: ☿</note> is to be refined into Gold <note>original: ☉</note> or Silver <note>original: ☽</note>.

After the **mercury** <note>original: ☿</note> has been well purified / one amalgamates it with cupelled **silver** <note>original: ☽</note> <term>cupellation</term> <note>A refining process where ores are treated under high heat to separate noble metals like silver from base metals.</note> / which has been stripped of its **copper** <note>original: ♀</note> soul through **salt** <note>original: 🜔; likely referring to vitriol in this context</note> and **sal ammoniac** <note>original: 🜹</note> / onto this amalgam one pours **sulfur** / which has been made into an oil through the **salt** <note>original: 🜔</note> of the Earth / as shall follow below / rub everything well together / until it has become a black powder / which one puts into a well-luted <term>luting</term> <note>Sealing a vessel with a clay-like paste to make it airtight and heat-resistant.</note> crucible / so that one can hold together all the more heat / which is required / to bring this composition into action / and to unite all its parts well / one maintains it thus with its fire for 14 days / and after it has cooled of its own accord / one takes out the matter / which will represent the form of a tree-moss / and will be as light as a feather / then one adds new **mercury** <note>original: ☿</note> to it / and places it again in a **suppression-fire** <term>suppression fire</term> <note>A method of heating where the crucible is completely covered or "smothered" by hot coals to ensure even, intense heat.</note> for 14 days / as before / and repeats this work until the crucible has become completely full / then one takes it out of the fire / and puts some of the matter on a red-hot copper plate / and if it glows without giving off smoke / then it will be perfectly cooked / and will have swallowed enough of the **mercury** <note>original: ☿</note>: In case it should still smoke / then one must put it again into a large crucible with new **mercury** <note>original: ☿</note> /

<summary>This page describes a lengthy alchemical process to "fix" mercury by repeatedly heating it with silver and sulfur in a sealed crucible. The goal is to create a stable, non-volatile substance that no longer produces smoke when heated, indicating it has been "perfectly cooked."</summary>

and apply fire from above / and continue with this work for so long / until it has been brought to the intended quality: Finally, throw it into a **silver-bath**, namely into an equal weight of molten **silver** <note>original: ☽</note> / where one will find the matter before them as a fine silver-like metal, stable in all tests.

### The Sulfur Oil.

One takes common **sulfur** <note>original: 🜍</note> and **sal ammoniac** <note>original: 🜔; an ammonium salt used as a flux</note> in equal parts <note>original: ana</note>, melts them together gradually in a flask into a **mass**, which is then poured while hot onto a marble stone and left for 24 hours in a damp place; thus it will turn into an oil, which is then freed from its **dregs** <note>original: fæcibus</note> through **decantation** <term>decantation</term> <note>The process of carefully pouring off a liquid to leave the solid sediment behind.</note>; and this can then be used for the aforementioned work.

### No. 13.
### A True Coagulation of Mercury.

Take a **mercury** <note>original: Mercurium</note> that has been outwardly purified of all blackness; place it into a small box made of fine silver, which must be tightly closed with its lid. Set this box with the **mercury** <note>original: Mercurio</note> into a pot filled with crushed **lead** <note>original: ♄</note>, so that the box is placed in the center; let it flow <note>The author means to keep the lead in a molten state.</note> for 24 hours, and the **mercury** <note>original: ☿</note> will be **congealed** <note>original: congeliret; turned from a liquid into a solid</note>.

Take <note>This "Take" completes the sentence started on the previous page.</note> three [ingredients] well pounded together / and 5 ounces of skimmed honey / and 10 ounces of sour whey / which has been distilled five times through a filter / and 15 ounces of human urine / mix all these together / and dissolve <note>original: solvir</note> it for 2 days in horse dung <note>original: fimo Equino</note>, then place it on warm ashes / so that it gently solidifies <note>original: coagulire</note> for 2 days in an open glass. After that, dissolve it and let it stand in horse manure / and seal the glass well. etc.

With this water, one solidifies **mercury** <note>original: ☿</note> without any sublimation <term>sublimation</term> <note>The process of heating a substance until it turns to vapor and then becomes solid again.</note>, and without any "killing" <note>In alchemy, "killing" or *mortificatio* refers to the process of stripping a metal of its external properties to reach its inner essence.</note> with a perfect stability. And with this water, you dissolve all **bodies** <note>original: Corpora; meaning solid metals</note>, and prepare them into pure **silver** <note>original: ☽</note> / also with this, every **spirit** <note>original: Spiritus; volatile alchemical substances like mercury or sulfur</note> is strengthened to withstand the fire / because of its quite wonderful power and might / to dissolve the passage / through the trials of all the salts / with **gold** <note>original: ☉</note> and also **silver** <note>original: ☽</note> / and this procedure is not to be bought for any amount of money / because of its virtue and truth.

Take 1 pound of beech ashes / 1 pound of **sal ammoniac** <term>sal ammoniac</term> <note>original: 🜔; ammonium chloride, frequently used in alchemy as a flux or to prepare "strong waters."</note> / and 2 pounds of **unslaked lime** <note>calcium oxide; a caustic substance that reacts strongly with water.</note> / all finely crushed / and pour boiling water upon them in a small cask / so that it stands a hand's breadth above them / and let it stand for 8 days / stirring it every day.

Afterward, take 1 pound of **vitriol** <term>vitriol</term> <note>original: 🜓; a sulfate of a metal, like iron or copper, often used for its corrosive properties.</note> / and just as much **copper** <note>original: ♀</note> / and calcinate <term>calcinate</term> <note>To heat a substance to a high temperature in the air to oxidize it or reduce it to a powder.</note> them together. After that, also take 1 pound of **gold** <note>original: ☉</note> / and 1 pound of **sulfur** <note>original: 🜍</note> / and put these 4 items into a good black pot / that can withstand the fire / and pour the lye <note>The alkaline liquid prepared in the first step.</note> over them / always adding it gradually / and thus let it simmer constantly and gently / so that it becomes a thick mash or paste. Afterward, test it with a small iron rod like this: Thrust the small rod into the mash / and hold it with the mash over the fire / if the mash on the rod burns / then it has not simmered long enough / pour more lye upon it / and let it simmer better / until the mash no longer burns. Afterward, pour the mash into a crucible / let it flow <note>melt</note> well / then pour it into a shard <note>a shallow ceramic dish or fragment</note> / and let it solidify / when it has solidified / take this same material and crush it into a fine powder / take 2 loth <note>A German unit of weight; 1 loth is approximately 15–16 grams.</note> of it / and 1 loth of **mercury** <note>original: ☿</note> / set it upon the fire / let them work together / and so the **mercury** coagulates <term>coagulates</term> <note>Changes from a liquid to a solid state.</note> into true **silver** <note>original: ☽</note> / and is fixed <term>fixed</term> <note>original: fix; in alchemy, a "fixed" substance is stable under heat and will not evaporate.</note>.

If you wish, however, to make **copper** <note>original: ♀</note> white like **silver** <note>original: ☽</note> / then take 2 loth of the powder described above / and 1 loth of **copper** / let it melt / and so the **copper** will become white. Just let it simmer well / the work is correct.

### No. 16.
### To transform **Silver** <term>Silver</term> <note>original symbol: ☽</note> / so that it also contains much **Gold** <term>Gold</term> <note>original symbol: ☉</note>.

One takes arsenic, **fixed gold** <note>original: ☉ fixum; gold that has been chemically treated to withstand extreme heat without vaporizing.</note>, **antimony** <note>original symbol: ♁</note>, and **iron filings** <note>original symbol: ♂</note> / all well mixed together / and melted with **tin-ash** <note>calcined tin or tin oxide, used as a flux or polishing agent</note> in a very strong fire / so that it flows like water; when the mass has become cold / it is turned into powder / and the impurities are washed away from it. Then the powder is dried again / and melted once more into metal. This operation is repeated until the **tin** <note>original symbol: ♃</note> has become very beautiful and clean / and that it has taken on the weight / the sound / and all the other properties and qualities of **silver** <note>original symbol: ☽</note> / so that afterward / when it is brought to the **cupel** <term>cupel</term> <note>original: Capelle; a shallow, porous dish made of bone ash used in assaying to separate precious metals from base metals.</note> / it will be found mixed with much **gold** <note>original symbol: ☉</note>.

### No. 17.
### To prepare **fixed Silver** <note>original: Lunam fixam; Latin for "fixed Moon," referring to silver that has been treated to resist the heat of the furnace or the action of acids.</note>.

Take 1 part fine **cupelled silver** <note>highly refined silver purified on a cupel</note> / 4 parts **lead** <note>original symbol: ♄</note> / let them flow <note>melt</note> together / then pour it onto 4 **loth** <term>loth</term> <note>A traditional German unit of weight, approximately 14.6 grams or half an ounce.</note> of crushed **sulfur** <note>original symbol: 🜍</note> / so that it becomes brittle / wash the sulfur away / and take an equal weight of **Venetian glass** <note>A high-quality clear glass often used as a flux to lower the melting point of metals.</note> / and of **precipitated steel** <note>iron that has been chemically dissolved and then settled out as a fine powder</note> / put these well mixed into a **flask** <note>original: Kolben; a glass or ceramic vessel used for heating</note> / let it stand in flux in the **wind-furnace** <term>wind-furnace</term> <note>original: Wind-Ofen; a furnace designed to draw a strong draft of air to reach very high temperatures.</note> for 4 or 5 hours / then let it cool / break the flask / and you will find the **silver** <note>original symbol: ☽</note> and **lead** <note>original symbol: ♄</note> at the bottom as a **regulus** <term>regulus</term> <note>original: König (King); the metallic button or mass that settles at the bottom of a crucible after the impurities have been separated into the slag.</note> and a lump / refine this on the **assaying dish** <note>original: Test; a shallow vessel used to refine or test the purity of metals.</note> / and you have thus well prepared the **fixed silver** <note>original symbol: ☽ fixa</note>.

### No. 18.
### Purification of Silver. <note>original: Purgatio Lunæ</note>

Take 4 **lots** <term>lot</term> <note>a historical German unit of weight, roughly 15 grams or half an ounce</note> of **calamine** <note>original: Galmey; a zinc ore used in brass making and alchemy</note> / 5 lots of raw **antimony** <note>original symbol: ♁</note> / 2 lots of **brick dust** <note>original: Ziegel-Mehl; used as a dry medium in cementation processes</note> / and 24 lots of common salt. Grind everything small together / take the **silver** <note>original: ☽ corporaliter; referring to silver in its physical, metallic state</note>, place it inside, sealed with **lute** <term>lute</term> <note>a fire-resistant clay or paste used to seal alchemical vessels</note>, let it flow for 6 or 8 hours / do this with fresh powder seven more times / this is an inexpressible purification for **p. c. p.** <note>likely an abbreviation for *pro conservatione perfectionis*, Latin for "for the preservation of perfection"</note> and it will no longer be attacked by any **nitric acid** <note>original: Aq. fort. (Aqua fortis), literally "strong water," the primary acid used to dissolve silver</note>.

### No. 19.
### Another Purification, through which the Silver becomes fixed for Confirmation, and attains the Weight of Gold.

Take 3 **pounds** <note>original symbol: ℔</note> of common salt / put it into a **flask** <term>flask</term> <note>original: Kolben; a long-necked distillation vessel</note> / let it flow well / and put 3 pounds of crushed raw **antimony** <note>original symbol: ♁</note> into it / and let it boil thus for three hours / but first also put in 20 lots of **iron filings** <note>original symbol: ♂-Feile</note> / afterward take the matter and grind it small / then take 2 parts of **lead** <note>original symbol: ♄</note> / and 1 part of this powder / let them melt into each other / thus the **antimonial lead** is prepared. Now take 1 part of **silver** <note>original symbol: ☽</note> / as fine as it is / which has previously been **cemented** <term>cementation</term> <note>a process of heating a metal with powdered reagents to alter its properties without melting the metal</note> 5 times with **vitriol** <note>original symbol: 🜔; sulfate minerals</note> and **sal ammoniac** <note>original symbol: 🜹; ammonium chloride</note> / and 1 $\frac{1}{2}$ parts of the prepared **lead** <note>original symbol: ♄</note> / let them drive into each other on the **cupel** <term>sherd</term> <note>original: Scherben; a ceramic dish used for high-heat refining</note> / until it turns well to slag / afterward **cupel** it completely on the **test** <term>test</term> <note>original: Teste; a large, porous dish used for refining precious metals</note> / take that / and an equal amount of the **antimonial lead** / and cupel it again / as before / do this four times / thus the **silver** <note>original: Luna</note> is purified / to such a high degree / that it is fixed for **confirmation** <note>the final stabilization of a metal's new properties</note> / and it has the weight of **gold** / which confirms it.

<summary>This page outlines two specific alchemical procedures for the "purgation" or intensive purification of silver. These processes aim to make the silver resistant to acid and increase its density and stability to mimic the characteristics of gold.</summary>
<keywords>Silver purification, Antimony, Calamine, Cupellation, Fixed Silver, Alchemical symbols, Nitric acid resistance, Weight of Gold</keywords>

->No. 20.<-

### NB. Another Purification of Silver for Stabilization. It is the Highest Art of Cleansing.

Take 2 **lots** <term>lot</term> <note>a historical German unit of weight, roughly 15 grams</note> of fine **silver** <note>original symbol: ☽ (Luna)</note> / and 3 lots of **bismuth** / refine them into one another on the **cupel** <term>cupel</term> <note>original: Test; a shallow, porous bowl used to refine precious metals</note> until the **flash** <note>original: Blick; the sudden brightening of the metal’s surface when impurities are finally removed</note> occurs 28 times / thus the silver will collapse with its brilliance / and rise into the highest **sphere of the Sun** <note>a metaphor for the silver attaining the color or quality of gold</note> / when you have accomplished this / take the silver with 5 times its weight in **antimony** <note>original symbol: ♁</note> / just as it comes from the **ore** / prepare with it **layer upon layer** <term>S. S. S.</term> <note>abbreviation for the Latin *stratum super stratum*</note> in a pointed **flask** / and let it flow strongly in the **blast furnace** <term>wind-oven</term> <note>a furnace designed to reach high temperatures via a natural or bellows-driven draft</note> for 1 hour / then a **regulus** <note>original: König (king); the metallic button that settles at the bottom of a crucible</note> will settle / refine it by itself / so you have **gold elevated in color, that is, colored** <note>original: ☉ elevatum colore, id est, Coloratum</note> / do this a second time / and test it with its old **lead** <note>original symbol: ♄ (Saturn)</note>. For what remains there / is preserved in all tests / do not cease / until you infuse it with the highest degree / through the art of this revelation.

->No. 21.<-

### Secret for an Amalgam of Tin and Mercury. <note>original: Arcanum auf ein Amalgama ♃ & Mercurii</note>

Take **antimony** <note>original symbol: ♁</note> / **sulphur** <note>original symbol: 🜍</note> / half a **pound** <note>original symbol: ℔ ß</note> of each. **Vitriol** <note>original symbol: 🜔</note> that has been **calcined** <term>calcined</term> <note>heated to a high temperature to remove moisture or volatile components</note> / and 4 lots of **verdigris** <note>original: Spangrün; the green crust that forms on copper, used as a reagent</note> / everything rubbed together finely / and **sublimated** <term>sublimated</term> <note>a process where a solid is heated into a vapor and then re-solidified to purify it</note> in an earthen sublimation vessel / use this sulphur / and **fix** <term>fix</term> <note>to stabilize a volatile substance so it no longer evaporates when heated</note> it together with **oil of tartar** <term>Oleo Tartari</term> <note>a concentrated, liquid solution of potassium carbonate</note> (the salt of which has been **sharpened** <note>original: acuiret; meaning to increase its chemical potency or corrosiveness</note> 6 times with **quicklime** <note>original: Calce viva</note> / and afterwards allowed to liquefy in a cellar /) / until no more smoke is noticed from the sulphur / then it is ready to be worked with.

Now take fine **English tin** / 4 **lots** <term>lot</term> <note>a historical German unit of weight, roughly 15 grams</note> / and 8 lots of **mercury** <note>original symbol: ☿</note> / **amalgamate** it / afterward let it sit in a **crucible** <term>crucible</term> <note>a heat-resistant container used for melting materials at high temperatures</note> in smoking heat / apply 2 lots of the previously prepared powder to it / let it flow for **two and a half** <note>original: drittehalb; an archaic German fractional term meaning the third is only a half, thus 2.5</note> hours / this results in very high-quality **silver** <note>original symbol: ☽ (Luna)</note> containing much **gold** <note>original symbol: ☉ (Sol)</note>.

## Chapter IX.

### A beautiful little work, in which it is correctly taught how to purify the metals and transform them into gold and silver.

It is to be known / that **Nature** from the beginning intended in the earth to make all **metallic bodies** <term>bodies</term> <note>original: Leichname (corpses); in alchemy, this refers to the solid, "dead" state of metals that must be "resurrected" or perfected</note> into **gold** <note>original symbol: ☉</note> and **silver** <note>original symbol: ☽</note>; and that this does not happen / comes perhaps from an impure **earthly matter** <note>original symbol: ♁ (Antimony); here used to represent the crude, unrefined minerals or "dross" mixed with the metal</note> / which is mixed with the **mercury** <note>original symbol: ☿</note> / or from the stinking soil / in which they come together / in the same way as a child in its mother's womb receives sickness / either from impure seed / or even if the **womb** <note>original: Matrix</note> is already quite pure / the child nevertheless becomes sick from the place / where it lies in its mother / which is stinking and impure. In the same way is the nature of the bodies / that come out of the earth. Now you should know / that there is a difference between all bodies / which comes from various types of **sulfur** / which is nothing other than the moisture of the earth.

If the sulfur is black and soft / and like

...flows back during the **cooking** <note>original: Kochen; in alchemy, this refers to "digestion," the natural heat within the earth that matures minerals</note> in its veins/ then a mixture occurs by the power of a star/ which is called **Saturn** <note>original symbol: ♄</note>. This one governs **lead**/ for from the mixture and the star’s influence, lead grows in the earth.

If the **sulfur** <note>original: Schwefel; considered one of the two primary components of all metals</note> is white and cloudy/ and moves during the cooking into its veins/ then a mixture is formed/ from which **tin** then grows in the earth/ by the power of a star/ which is called **Jupiter** <note>original symbol: ♃</note>.

If the sulfur is black and hard/ and occurs during the cooking in its veins/ then a mixture is formed by the influence of the planet **Mars** <note>original symbol: ♂</note>/ and it becomes **iron**.

If the sulfur is red/ clear and bright and pure/ and flows back during the pure cooking in the veins of the earth/ then a mixture occurs by the influence of the **Sun** <note>original symbol: ☉; representing Gold</note>/ and **gold** <note>original symbol: ☉</note> grows in the earth.

If the sulfur is red and cloudy/ during the cooking from the mixture of the star **Venus** <note>original symbol: ♀</note>/ then **copper** grows in the earth.

You should also know/ that **quicksilver** <note>original: Koch; likely a variant or error for "Quecksilber"</note> also grows in the earth/ from the power of a star/ which is called **Mercury** <note>original symbol: ☿</note>.

But if the sulfur is white and clear and bright/ and flows back during the pure cooking of its veins/ then a mixture arises/ from which **silver** <note>original symbol: ☽</note> grows in the earth/ by the power and dominion of a star/ which is called the **Moon**.

These are the seven planets/ by whose power and dominion the **bodies** <term>bodies</term> <note>original: Leichname; literally "corpses," referring to the solid metallic form</note> come/ and are born in the earth.

Now, as we wish to proceed to the **transformation of the corpses** <note>original: Verwandlung der Leichnam; in alchemy, "corpses" refers to the solid, "dead" state of base metals before they are revived or transformed by the alchemist</note>/ it is necessary to know what belongs to this art. I will therefore tell you the **Bodies** and **Spirits** <note>original: Corpora und Spiritus; "Bodies" are solid metals, while "Spirits" are volatile substances that can evaporate or "flee" the fire</note> that are required for the art. There are seven things which we call **Bodies** in the art. These are: Gold, Silver, Tin, Iron, Lead, Copper, and Mercury <note>original: Koch; likely a variant for Quicksilver/Mercury</note>.

Furthermore, there are five things which we call the **Spirits** of the aforementioned bodies; namely: Mercury, Realgar <note>original: Rüßgelb; a ruby-red arsenic sulfide</note>, Sulfur, Orpiment <note>original: Operment; a yellow arsenic sulfide</note>, and Sal Ammoniac <note>original: Salmiac; ammonium chloride, used to clean metals and aid sublimation</note>.

These five Spirits transform the bodies into **Red** and **White**/ that is / into **Gold** <term>☉</term> and **Silver** <term>☽</term>: And yet not by themselves / unless they are brought to it and prepared with various **penance and medicine** <note>original: Buß und Arzney; metaphorical terms for the chemical processes of purification and stabilization</note>/ so that they do not flee the fire. And when they remain steady in the fire / they perform wonderful things. For they transform Iron, **Copper** <term>♀</term> / **Lead** <term>♄</term> / and in short, all "corpses" into **Gold** <term>☉</term> and **Silver** <term>☽</term> / which becomes better in all things than that which Nature has given in the earth.

For otherwise, the iron made by Art does not attract the **magnet** <note>original: Adamant; while this can mean diamond, in the context of iron it refers to the lodestone or magnet</note> to itself: And the **Gold** <term>☉</term>/ when it is made by Art/ makes the heart joyful and also drives away **leprosy**: And wounds that are swollen/ it heals/ which the natural gold otherwise does not do. And in all other things/ colors/ tests, and works/ it lasts much longer. From the fifth, however, one makes a powder which they call the Mai- <note>This catchword "Mei-" likely leads to "Meisterstück" or "Meisterpulver," meaning the Masterpiece or Master Powder</note>

**Master Elixir.** Elixir is an Arabic word / and is translated into German / as a leaven <note>original: Urhab; refers to a fermenting agent or starter like sourdough</note> or yeast <note>original: Gerben; in this historical context, it refers to the catalyst that causes dough to rise or "improves" the batch</note>. For just as bread improves or rises from good leaven / so too do the **Bodies** <note>the metals</note> rise / and transform themselves into **Gold** <term>☉</term> and **Silver** <term>☽</term> by these five **Spirits**, and most of all by the **Cook** <note>original: Koch; the author's specific term for Mercury/Quicksilver</note> / for it is the origin of all **Bodies**.

You must also have knowledge of several others / which indeed do not have the power / to transform the bodies / but nevertheless help the five / so that they become prepared / and so that one can better turn them to **Water** <term>▽</term> / and with their **Water** <term>▽</term> one mixes the **ashes of the corpses** <note>the powdered, calcined remains of base metals</note> / so that they gain a moisture / which then governs the corpses. And how one should prepare each thing / I will teach you one after the other.

->I.<-
### Quicksilver or the Cook.

This is nothing other / than a viscous and seeping **Water** <term>▽</term> / gathered through the earth / and well-tempered heat / well-collected through the veins of the earth / until the moisture is mixed equally with the dryness / and for that reason it flees so easily in the fire / and yet sticks to nothing / as its seeping nature is well-tempered by the dryness / and does not allow sticking / and is, with **Sulfur**, a mother of all **corpses** <note>metals</note>.

One finds it in the mountains / and sometimes in old lakes. Its nature is warm and moist /

### 2.
### Sulfur.

This is the moisture of the earth / which produces a well-tempered heat within its veins / until it becomes hard and dry; And when it becomes dry / it is called **Sulfur**. ### 5.
### Sal Ammoniac.
<note>Ammonium chloride; a salt used in alchemy to clean metals and act as a flux</note>

Take 1 part common salt / 3 parts prepared urine / and 1 part soot / boil all of this in a kettle / until the urine boils down / thereafter pour more urine onto it / and boil it again therein. Do this for 7 hours / then let it dry / and sublimate it <note>original: sublimirs; the process of heating a solid until it becomes a vapor and then collects again as a solid to purify it</note> / which is as much as to say / that you refine it.

### 9.
### Alum.

I will not conceal this from you, for it is also very useful to this art; prepare it thus: Take as much of it as you can find for sale and put it in a pot; fill it to the half-way point and seal the pot very well; set it in the furnace and give it a gentle fire until it warms up, then increase the fire until it begins to glow, and let it stand in the heat for one day; afterwards, when it has cooled, take it out, and you will find the alum snow-white; keep that, for from it one makes <term>sal ammoniac</term> <note>symbol: 🜔; a salt used as a flux or to "cleanse" metals</note> for <term>silver</term> <note>symbol: ☽; the Moon, representing silver in alchemical shorthand</note>.

->10.<-

### Tartar / <term>🜝</term> <note>original: Weinstein; the symbol 🜝 represents Tartar, the potassium bitartrate crusts found in wine barrels.</note>.

It is also a substance that provides great help to this art, and is prepared for our work in this manner: <term>Take</term> <note>symbol: ℞; the traditional recipe abbreviation for "Recipe"</note> <term>Tartar</term>, calcine it with a strong fire until it is truly white, which can be done very conveniently in a brick kiln or a potter's kiln. Then keep it until I teach you how to turn it into <term>water</term> <note>symbol: 🜄; here referring to "Oil of Tartar," a liquid formed when the calcined salt absorbs moisture from the air</note>, for it is one of those substances that keeps all <term>bodies and spirits</term> <note>original: Corpora und Spiritus; "bodies" are solid metals, while "spirits" are volatile substances like mercury or sulfur</note> in the fire and makes them stable <note>original: stete; meaning "fixed" or resistant to evaporation in the heat</note>.

->11.<-

### Copper-green / or Verdigris.

Copper-green is good for this art above all things, and it is made thus: Take <term>copper</term> <note>symbol: ♀; the symbol for the planet Venus, representing copper</note> sheets and hang them over strong vinegar in a pot, and seal it well, and place it in a warm location and let it stand for 3 or 4 weeks. Then take them out, and you will find copper-green hanging on the sheets; remove that very cleanly, and set the sheets up again as before until the sheets have all become copper-green. And note, if you wish to have a great deal of it, do it thus: Take honey and warm it; take <term>Roman Vitriol</term> <note>symbol: 🜔; likely copper sulfate or "blue vitriol"</note>, <term>copper</term>, and lime, and pour these together into the honey; stir them together until it becomes thick, and smear it on both sides of the sheets, and hang them over the vinegar as before. Do with the copper-green in all respects as I have taught you regarding <term>Atrament</term> <note>a general term for vitriols or black pigments</note>, and it will become a red color.

<summary>This page provides recipes for two essential alchemical additives: calcined tartar, used for "fixing" volatile substances, and verdigris (copper-green), produced using vinegar and honey.</summary>

### 12.
### Cinnabar.

This is also one of those substances useful in this art, and it is made thus: Take 3 parts <term>antimony</term> <note>original: Antimonium / Spießglas; symbol: ♁</note>, 1 part <term>mercury</term> <note>original: Mercurius / Quecksilber; symbol: ☿</note>, rub them together, and put them into a glass with a narrow neck. Coat the glass all over with <term>lute</term> <note>original: Luto; a specialized alchemical clay or paste used to seal vessels against high heat</note>, and let it dry well. Place it on the furnace where the spirits are separated; seal the glass well and give it a gentle <term>fire</term> <note>symbol: △</note>. Burn it for a whole day, or for as long as it takes until you see red smoke rising out of the top. Then stop and let it cool. Break the glass, and you will find good cinnabar.

### 13.
### White Lead and Minium.

They are made thus: Take lead sheets and set them over sharp vinegar in a strong pot made for this purpose, and seal it well with <term>lute</term>. Proceed in all things as you were taught regarding copper-green. Keep that, for you must make <term>minium</term> <note>original: Minium; also known as red lead, a bright orange-red pigment</note> from it, which is useful for the art. But make the minium from it thus: Rub the white lead very well on a stone and make <term>small pellets</term> <note>original: Strützelein; small conical cakes or hand-formed lumps</note> from it, and place them in an earthenware pot that is shaped like a basin...

Some are separated with a strong fire, such as <term>marcasite</term> <note>original: Marcasitam; an iron sulfide mineral</note>, <term>magnesia</term> <note>original: Magnesiem; here likely referring to manganese ore or a similar mineral</note>, etc., which we do not require here. Some are separated in a gentle fire, such as <term>tin</term> <note>symbol: ♃</note>; some with a mild fire, such as <term>mercury</term> <note>original: Quecksilber; symbol: ☿</note>. This is a masterly concept: to separate from the <term>spirits</term> <note>the volatile alchemical components</note> that which is extraneous to them, for they often mix with things that are unlike their own nature. And one separates them so that they become like <term>calx</term> <note>original: Kalck; a powdery metallic oxide</note> that is burnt from <term>pebbles</term> <note>original: Kießling; likely flint or quartz stones</note> or other white stones. And if every <term>salt</term> <note>symbol: ⊖</note> is well-prepared from these things, the spirits will be made pure by it. They become even more impure than the <term>corpses</term> <note>original: Leichnam; the solid, non-volatile residues left after heating</note>, for they contain all the previous <term>sulfur</term> <note>original: Schwefel</note> that flies out with them and contaminates them; and therefore, if one separates them from <term>tin</term> <note>symbol: ♃</note> and <term>lead</term> <note>symbol: ♄</note>, one finds them black and impure. For this reason, it is better to separate them from these things that are unlike them, such as the sulfur they contain. The method for separating excess water and moisture from <term>mercury</term> <note>symbol: ☿</note> is to mix it with other substances until it is no longer recognizable or distinguishable, and then place it over a gentle fire; then the moisture flies away with it, as will be taught hereafter.

...nothing more than this: a pulverization <note>original: Pulverung; in alchemy, this usually refers to calcination, where a substance is reduced to powder by heat</note> by the power of fire upon every thing, by depriving it of the moisture that it previously held and which bound it together. One pulverizes the <term>corpses</term> <note>original: Leichnam; the solid, non-volatile residues left after a substance has been heated or distilled</note> so that they become pure from their filth. Likewise, one also pulverizes the <term>spirits</term> <note>original: Geister; the volatile components that rise during heating</note> for this and many other reasons. Firstly, so that the corpses lose their impure taste <note>the chemical properties or impurities detected by alchemists through tasting</note>, and without this, it cannot be. Thus, pulverization was discovered so that one might harden the moist corpses therein. For it is plainly seen that they become hard in this sense, especially <term>tin</term> <note>original: Zinn</note>. However, the reason one pulverizes the spirits is so that they become <term>stable</term> <note>original: stete; meaning "fixed" or no longer volatile in the fire</note> all the sooner, and convert themselves into "water" <note>likely referring to deliquescence, where a powder absorbs moisture from the air to become a liquid</note> all the better. For some burnt things are stable in the fire and also turn into water more readily than an unburnt thing.

### Coagulation <note>original Latin: Coagulatio</note>

Thus, purification and hardening are nothing other than the deprivation of moisture from a watery or moist thing, restoring it to a hard mass. For it is often found that <term>mercury</term> <note>original: Quecksilber</note> is hardened and purified, and that the watery parts it contains are driven from it by the moisture and made hard. One hardens the <term>salt</term> <note>original: Koch; likely referring to common salt or a saline substance</note> in this way and "kills" <note>original: tödtet; a term for chemically altering or "fixing" a substance so it no longer reacts or evaporates</note> it, so that one burns it intensely and with force. ### Fixation <note>original Latin: Fixatio; the process of making a volatile substance stable so that it no longer evaporates when heated</note>

Thus, fixation <note>original: Bestätigung</note> is nothing other than a pleasing purification of volatile <note>original: fliehenden; literally "fleeing"</note> things through the fire. The reason this was discovered is so that every transformation and change of one substance into another may be complete and stable <note>original: stet</note>, and not revert. One makes the imperfect bodies <note>unrefined metals or chemical solids</note> stable through their pulverization, for they are thus released from their volatile and corrupt moisture. Sulfur <note>original: Schwefel</note> and orpiment <note>original: Rüßgelb; a yellow arsenic sulfide mineral used as a pigment and in alchemy</note> are made stable in two ways: First, by separating them in their "waters" until they persist and do not flee. Second, one also makes the <term>spirits</term> stable with the "water of the corpses" <note>liquid extracted from the solid residues of a previous distillation</note> or with oil of tartar <note>original: Weinstein-Oel; a saturated solution of potassium carbonate</note>, as I will teach you hereafter.

Thus, solution <note>original: Erlösung; here meaning dissolution or liquefaction</note> and flow <note>original: Fliessiung</note> are nothing other than the transformation into "water" of every thing that is pulverized. The reason this was discovered is so that its outward nature might become its inward nature, and also so that one may better refine the <term>corpses</term> and the <term>spirits</term>; in this way, all its filth is refined and purged from it. Pay attention: one may turn a thing into water in two ways, either with heat or with moisture—such as in a cold pit or in a cellar—as I will teach you hereafter.

Thus, **purification** <note>original German: Läuterung; in this context, it refers primarily to distillation and refining</note> is nothing other than the rising of watery vapor into its vessel. There are many ways of preparation: some purification is performed with fire, and some without fire. That which is performed with <term>fire</term> <note>original: △, the alchemical symbol for fire</note> is of two kinds:

One is outward <note>meaning distillation where the vapor rises upward</note>, such as through a "hood" or an **alembic** <note>the upper part of a still, used to catch and condense vapors</note>, where one burns substances out together. Why was this discovered? It was so that one might purify a <term>water</term> <note>original: ▽, the alchemical symbol for water; refers to any liquid distillate</note> from its dregs or residues <note>original: Gerben; literally "tannins," referring here to harsh or bitter impurities</note>, and to preserve it so that it does not rot. For we see that every thing that is pure remains beautiful and clear. It was also discovered specifically so that one might dry the **spirits** <note>volatile substances</note> and the **powders**, if this should be required; meanwhile, one gains pure and clear water that leaves no dregs or filth behind, from which the spirits or powders might otherwise receive impurity.

But why was it devised to purify "contrary-wise" <note>original: widerwerts; likely referring to distillation by descent (distillatio per descensum), where vapors are forced downward or sideways rather than upward</note>? This is because that which is pure oil in its nature has an exuding quality; in its "flowering" state, it cannot be forced upward, as is seen with **petroleum** <note>original: Petroleo; naturally occurring rock oil</note>.

Thus, purification with the **felt** <note>filtration through fabric or felt wicks</note> is nothing other than the preparation of hard and non-fluid things, such as a white wax. From this, one should note that it was devised so that nothing can enter the "bodies" <note>the solid matter or metallic base</note> because of their "thirstiness"—which desires to be transformed—until the substance is paired and becomes fluid, so that it may enter the "corpse" <note>original: Leichnam; the solid residue or the "dead" matter waiting to be reanimated by the spirit</note>.

## 2. How one should refine the <term>Sulfur</term> <note>original: sulfur</note>:
Take and grind it small / and boil it in strong vinegar for a whole day / and skim it off the top quite beautifully. Now you shall learn how to make the spirits, which have been separated, stable <note>original: "stete"; in alchemy, this refers to 'fixation,' or making a substance resistant to heat</note>, so that they may remain in the fire, and join themselves to the body <note>original: "Leichnam," literally 'corpse'; refers to the solid, non-volatile material or 'base' of the substance</note>, and unite with it. ### How to make the separated spirits stable and fixed in two ways.

#### I. Method. <note>original: "Modus"</note>

Take the spirit which you desire, and add no other thing to it, whether of the 1st part or 2nd part, and place it into a vessel that is made like this <image-desc>alchemical symbol for a sealed vessel or crucible, consisting of a circle with a horizontal line through the middle</image-desc>, in which spirits are made stable. It should be made of an excellent mass of clay and well-tempered with masterly adhesives. The vessel should be made the thickness of a finger. In this vessel, place your spirit and seal it thoroughly with a <term>lute</term> <note>original: "Luto"; a specialized airtight clay or cement used by alchemists to seal vessels against high heat</note>, and set it upon the separation-furnace. First give it a gentle fire, and once it has warmed up, increase the fire more and more, and burn it thus for a whole day. On the second day, turn the vessel over, with the bottom upwards, and burn it thus for the second day. This you shall do for nine days; then open the vessel and see whether the powder has become stable. Test it in this way: Lay a little of it upon a glowing coal; if the powder smokes, then it has not had enough time; but if it does not smoke, then it has had enough, and it is fixed. ## Chapter X.
### A Tincture by the Short Way.

Take your mercury <term>mercury: original symbol ☿; also known as quicksilver</term> which has been sublimated <note>sublimation is the process of heating a substance until it becomes a vapor and then solidifies again, used here for purification</note> nine times; grind this very fine. Put it into a Venetian glass, into a vial. Lay this, well-luted <note>original: "lutirt"; sealed with "lute," a clay or paste used to make laboratory joints airtight</note> with the matter, into a sand-bath <term>sand-bath: original "Sand-Capellen"; a container filled with sand heated from below to provide a steady, even temperature</term> in such a way that the vial lies with its belly halfway in the sand. The sand should be very hot, so that the sublimate flows within like an alkali salt oil <note>original: "Sal-alcali-Oel"</note> and sublimates upward. When it is all in the upper part, turn the vial over, so that the sublimate will sublimate upward again. This turning and sublimating must continue until the mercury-sublimate no longer rises and has become entirely fixed <note>original: "fix"; rendered non-volatile so that it no longer evaporates when heated</note>. This should be tested upon a glowing coal.

When it is now fixed, grind it fine and put

Do the same with the other metals, and know that this tincture also deeply colors all precious stones.

## Chapter XIII.
### A Beautiful Augment.

Take 2 parts fixed <term>copper</term> <note>original: "Venerem fixam"; copper that has been treated to withstand heat without oxidizing or vaporizing</note>, as the preparation shall be taught hereafter; 1 part fine gold purified through <term>mercury</term> <note>original symbol: ☿</note>; [and] 1 part red <term>sulfur</term> <note>original: "🜍 rubeum"</note>, whose preparation shall likewise follow below; let it flow <note>melt</note> well; then carry another 1 part sulfur into it, and more if it is necessary; the sulfur burns away entirely, but the copper-redness remains with the gold, which is colored to the highest degree.

To this gold, heightened in its color, add an equal weight of fixed <term>silver</term> <note>original symbol: ☽</note>, namely that which has been purified to the highest degree from its Venusian soul <note>original: "venerischen Anima"; referring to copper-like impurities or the "green" quality in silver</note> by <term>saltpeter</term> <note>original symbol: 🜔</note> and <term>sal ammoniac</term> <note>original symbol: 🜹</note>, etc.; this takes the exalted gold with it into permanent and fine gold.

### To Fix Copper.

->Take:<-
1 pound <note>original: "lbj."</note> copper; 1 pound each of mercury, saltpeter, and <term>tartar</term> <note>original symbol: 🜽</note>; [and] 8 Loth of <term>iron</term> filings <note>original symbol: ♂</note>; let it flow into a <term>regulus</term> <note>original: "Könige"; the metallic lump that settles at the bottom of a crucible after melting</note>. Let this regulus and an equal amount of copper flow together again into a regulus; crush the regulus, and let the mercury smoke away upon the <term>cupel</term> <note>original: "Treibscherben"; a shallow clay dish used in refining precious metals</note>; thus the mercury remains and is fixed <note>made non-volatile</note>. This is what is used for the <term>exaltation</term> <note>the process of improving the color and purity</note> of gold.

### Regarding the things necessary for cupellation.

### How one should prepare ashes for the tests and cupels.

The ash which is burned from linden, alder, fir, or spruce wood is the very best for this purpose. The reason is that such ash has little salt, sharpness, acidity, or bitterness, for which reason also no good lye can be boiled from it. For this is to be noted: regardless of which ash you may have, you must nevertheless thoroughly extract all <term>salt</term> <note>original symbol: ⊖</note>, acidity, and bitterness with hot <term>water</term> <note>original symbol: ▽</note>, which is done by constant boiling and pouring off. But to spare much effort, one can take that ash from which the lye has already been poured, and which is otherwise thrown away as the <term>spent ash</term> <note>German: Aeschrich; the residue left after the soluble salts have been leached out to make lye</note>. This ash...

<meta>The previous page introduced the importance of wood ash for making laboratory vessels, noting that linden, alder, and fir are best because they contain fewer salts. <header>❁ (0) ❁</header>

<term>Spent ash</term> <note>German: Aescherich; the residue left after the soluble salts (potash) have been leached out to make lye</note> can be taken and hot <term>water</term> <note>original symbol: ▽</note> poured through it several times in a lye-bag, so that all remaining salt and bitterness is removed from it. Then, take such ash and place it in a large, wide vessel. Pour plenty of clear flowing water upon it and stir it well together; in this way, the charcoal, wood, and straw will float to the top. These should be skimmed off until nothing more floats on the surface. Then stir it once more and reach with your hand to the bottom, and whatever you find there in the way of sand and stones, remove those as well; or, quickly pour the water together with the <term>suspended ash</term> <note>original: solvirten Aschen; referring to the fine particles held in suspension</note> into another clean vessel, so that the stones and sand remain behind. Let the other <term>water</term> <note>original symbol: ▽</note> together with the ash stand overnight; in this way, the ash settles very finely and beautifully to the bottom, and the water floats again clear and pure on top. Pour that off and dry the ash very well; then, after it has been driven through a hair sieve, it is beautifully and well prepared.

If you now wish to turn the bones into beautiful white ash, it must be done in a pot, in a pot-

[pottery] or brick kiln; the pot must be new and left open. When they <note>The bones</note> are thus glowing hot and become completely translucent, they are generally burnt enough. ## How one should forge and make the tests and cupels.
<note>Tests (Testen) and cupels (Capellen) are shallow, porous vessels made of ash used by assayers and alchemists to separate precious metals like gold and silver from base metals like lead.</note>

Take as much of the prepared ash as you wish or require; moisten it with plain <term>water</term> <note>original symbol: ▽</note>—flowing <term>water</term> <note>original symbol: ▽</note> is the best—working it between the hands like wax. Then knead it into the <term>cupel-mold</term> <note>German: Capellen-Futter; the outer casing or frame used to shape the vessel</note>, which you should have previously greased well with <term>tallow</term> <note>German: Unschlit; rendered animal fat used here as a release agent</note>, so that once the cupels have been forged, they come out more easily and without tearing. When the cupel-mold is thus filled with the kneaded, moistened ash, place the stamp that belongs on top of it. The stamp should be made of hard wood and be rounded at the bottom, turned like a half-sphere. Strike it two or three good blows with the hammer, and in this way, forge the hollows into them. And know that the harder the cupels are forged, the better and more useful they are; for they [cannot be] too hard and firm...

[taper] upwards and thin out, so that at the very top it is no more than half an <term>inch</term> <note>original: Zoll</note> thick. In the front of the furnace, before it is fired, a small door should be cut, approximately 4 inches wide and just as high. At the bottom of the furnace, where it meets the hearth, three **arched cut-outs** should be made: one at the back and one on each side. Each arched cut-out should be one inch high at its center, where it is highest. These are made for this reason: so that the air may escape between the furnace and the hearth during the <term>assaying</term> <note>original: Probieren; the metallurgical process of testing ore or alloy to determine its ingredient proportions and quality</note>. A small door should also be fired from <term>clay</term> <note>original: Läim; loam or potter's clay</note>, which is sized correctly and fits well into the cut-out door of the assay furnace. Thus the furnace is finished, the form of which is as follows:

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration of a Probier-Ofen (assay furnace). It is a trapezoidal, kiln-like structure with a flat, grated top. On the left side, there is a small arched opening (Thürlein) with a visible interior grate or door structure. The base shows the beginning of the arched cut-outs (Bogen-Schnitte) mentioned in the text.</image-desc>

The small <term>cupels</term> <note>original: Treib-Scherblein; literally "driving-shards," these are shallow, porous vessels used to separate precious metals from lead and impurities</note>, however, should also be baked from good potter's clay for use in such <term>assay furnaces</term> <note>original: Probier-Ofen</note>. They should be about one and a half inches wide in their circumference, half an inch high, and three and a half inches <note>original: vierdtehalb; an archaic German measurement meaning "the half before the fourth," or 3.5</note> thick, and deep enough to refine the ore within them. ### A good <term>Lutum Sapientiæ</term> <note>Latin: "Mud of Wisdom" or "Philosopher's Paste"; a heat-resistant cement used by alchemists and metallurgists to seal vessels or build furnaces</note>, from which the assay furnace can also be made.

Take a good clay that has been well sifted, washed, and purified; knead it with salt-water, and mix in <term>iron sinter</term> <note>original: Eisensinter; the scale or sparks that fly off iron during forging</note>, brick dust, and plenty of well-beaten <term>shear-wool</term> <note>original: Scheerwolle; small scraps of wool used as a binding fiber to prevent the clay from cracking as it dries</note> beaten into it and mixed thoroughly. This clay is very good, solid, and as strong as iron in the fire. If you wish to have it even better, so that it does not shrink at all and remains strong and firm in the fire—ringing like iron—then mix into the clay plenty of <term>iron scale</term> <note>original: Eisendach; likely a form of iron oxide or hammer scale</note>, which potters use to make pots and furnaces gray, and...

## Making the Best Testing Crucibles and Cupels.

The very best <term>testing crucibles</term> <note>original: Teste; vessels used for larger-scale refining</note> and <term>cupels</term> <note>original: Capellen; small, porous bowls used to separate precious metals from impurities</note> are made from good, clean <term>bone ash</term> <note>original: Bein-Aschen; calcium phosphate produced by burning bones, essential for absorbing lead oxide during refining</note>. These are beaten very firmly into a corresponding brass mold or ring, as mentioned previously. The bone ash should be moistened with ordinary water, though rain or river water is even better for this purpose. 

However, if one wishes to <term>refine</term> <note>original: abtreiben; the process of separating base metals like lead from precious metals</note> iron <note>original symbol: ♂ (Mars)</note>, copper <note>original symbol: ♀ (Venus)</note>, or similar ores upon them, where other common cupels often fail to hold up, one should moisten the ash with beaten egg whites; this makes them very firm and holds them together powerfully. 

Some practitioners make their testing crucibles inside flat earthenware shards. This is done so that if the crucible should crack or break open, the gold <note>original symbol: ☉ (Sun)</note>, silver <note>original symbol: ☽ (Moon)</note>, or whatever metal one is working with remains within the shard. Thus, one does not have to search for it long in the fire, nor is much of it lost. 

Some make the cupels and testing crucibles from <term>leached ash</term> <note>original: Sechen-Aschen; wood ash from which the lye has been extracted</note> and bone ash mixed together in <term>equal parts</term> <note>original: ana; a common alchemical and pharmaceutical term from the Greek 'ana' meaning 'of each'</note>. Others use one part of such leached ash to two or three parts of bone ash, according to each person's preference. Yet, the more bone ash used, the better the result. On the very top, however, one should place sifted ash made from the bones of calf heads and fish bones or skeletons, so that...

<summary>This page provides recipes for assaying vessels, emphasizing the use of bone ash and egg whites for durability. It describes techniques to prevent the loss of precious metals like gold and silver if a crucible fails during the high-heat refining process.</summary>
<keywords>assaying, cupels, bone ash, metallurgical symbols, gold refining, silver refining, testing crucibles, alchemical equipment</keywords>

...the metals do not stick or get lost within them.

### How to Make Good Crucibles.

For crucibles and all earthenware vessels, no earth is better than the potter’s clay from Zöllen or—here in Silesia—the clay from <term>Bunzlau</term> <note>Modern-day Bolesławiec; historically famous for its high-quality, heat-resistant stoneware</note>. This is suitable for all vessels intended for use in the fire, such as <term>crucibles</term>, <term>muffles</term> <note>original: Muffeln; semi-cylindrical chambers that protect a sample from direct flame while allowing heat to pass through</note>, <term>cupels</term> <note>original: Treib-Scherben; shallow vessels used in the process of separating precious metals from lead</note>, and <term>pots</term>.

If one cannot obtain such clays, however, one should make the following mixture: Take <term>iron sinter</term> or <term>hammer scale</term> <note>original: Hammerschlag; the black flakes of iron oxide that fall off hot iron during forging</note>, crush it into a fine flour, and wash the dust away. Take one part of this, one part of crushed <term>pot shards</term> or <term>brick dust</term>, and two parts of <term>iron scale</term> <note>original: Eisendach; likely a specific form of iron oxide or slag used for its refractory properties</note>. Mix these well together and have a potter fire them into a crucible or other vessel; these will then hold up very well in the heat of the fire.

If you wish to coat the outside of the vessels so that you may use them several times without them easily cracking or breaking in the fire, take <term>leached ash</term> <note>original: Sech-Asche; wood ash that has had its alkaline salts (lye) washed out</note> and <term>quicklime</term> <note>original: ungelöschten Kalck; a caustic substance produced by heating limestone</note> in equal parts. Moisten this with egg whites and rub it together into a thin paste. Coat the crucible with this one, two, or three times, but always allow it to dry thoroughly between layers until the coating is approximately as thick as the back of a knife.

<summary>This page provides recipes for creating durable, heat-resistant metallurgical vessels using specific regional clays or a mixture of iron scale and brick dust. It also describes a protective coating made of ash, lime, and egg white to extend the life of the crucibles.</summary>
<keywords>metallurgy, crucible making, Bunzlau clay, hammer scale, muffle, cupellation, furnace equipment, quicklime</keywords>

<meta>Continuing from the discussion on preparing heat-resistant vessels, this page shifts to the specific manual techniques—the "manipulations"—required for the process of refining precious metals using a furnace and cupels. The catchword "Man" links the end of the previous section to the word "Manipulationes."</meta>

## Necessary <term>Manipulations</term> <note>original: Manipulationes; the specific hand-movements and technical handling</note> to Know in <term>Refining</term> <note>original: Abtreiben; the process of cupellation, where impurities are absorbed by a porous vessel, leaving behind pure gold or silver</note>.

First, it is necessary to know how the <term>fire</term> <note>original symbol: Δ</note> should be governed so that one does not fail in the refining, nor lose <term>gold</term> <note>original symbol: ☉</note> or <term>silver</term> <note>original symbol: ☽</note> or whatever it may be. For example: One takes 1, 2, or more <term>cupels</term> <note>original: Capellen; small, shallow, porous bowls made of bone ash or clay</note> that are well made and not rough, as was taught before regarding their preparation: Place them into the <term>assay furnace</term> <note>original: Probier-Ofen</note> under the <term>muffle</term> <note>original: Muffel; a ceramic chamber that protects the samples from direct contact with fuel while allowing heat to circulate</note>, fill the furnace below and above with coals to more than halfway, and initially provide fire from above so that the furnace heats up well and the cupels within are well warmed and <term>tempered</term> <note>original: geädnet; conditioned or brought to a uniform heat to prevent cracking</note>. The intake hole by the muffle and the ash hole must be well closed off with glowing coals so that it heats up thoroughly everywhere.

When the cupels then glow well through and through and are in a state of full heat, one initially adds a very small amount of lead and observes whether it spits <note>original: sprüzet; pops or splashes, usually caused by moisture trapped in the cupel or insufficient heat</note>; if this happens, it is a sign that the cupel is not yet hot enough. Now, when the lead flows or "drives" <note>original: treibet; a term describing the movement of molten metal as it oxidizes and is absorbed</note> beautifully bright, one gradually adds the material, the <term>regulus</term> <note>original: König; the metallic "button" or mass resulting from a previous smelting step</note>, or whatever else one has for refining; let it flow and "drive" well in full heat. To ensure it does not cool down, the top hole where the cupels are placed must always be well closed with glowing coals.

<vocab>Manipulations, Refining, Fire, Gold, Silver, Cupels, Assay Furnace, Muffle, Coals, Lead, Regulus</vocab>

--- Page 228 ---
<meta>Continuing from the instructions on the initial heating of the cupels and the addition of lead, this page describes how to regulate the furnace heat and recognize the visual signs of successful purification (cupellation) of gold and silver.</meta>

(0)

One must also take good care that if the furnace happens to become too hot—which is indicated when one does not see the matter on the <term>cupel</term> <note>original: Capelle; a shallow, porous dish used to separate precious metals from base metals</note> flowing or "driving," and a mist appears above the cupels due to the great heat—then one must open up the coals a little, move the cupels forward, and gradually blow on them with <term>bellows</term> <note>original: Blasbalge oder Pucher</note> so that the process gets moving. This ensures that the <term>lead-smoke</term> <note>original: Bleyrauch; toxic litharge fumes released as lead oxidizes</note>, especially in <term>antimony-based operations</term> <note>original: Antimonialische Labores; chemical works involving the use of antimony to purify gold</note>, is driven away bit by bit, allowing the matter on the cupel to flow all the sooner.

When the process is then in a good flow or "driving," and begins to form <term>little flowers</term> <note>original: Blümlein; crystalline patterns on the surface of the molten metal</note>, which is a sign that such a sample contains <term>gold</term> <note>original symbol: ☉</note> or <term>silver</term> <note>original symbol: ☽</note>, then one must maintain and regulate the furnace and the fire at a moderate heat, so that the sample does not cool down but instead flows toward its <term>fineness</term> <note>original: Feine; the state of being pure or high-carat</note>.

When the <term>silver</term> <note>original symbol: ☽</note> has almost finished flowing and is about to become pure, it immediately covers itself with a red <term>fire-skin</term> <note>original: Feuer-Haut; a thin film of oxidized lead</note>. Immediately thereafter, when it is about to <term>brighten</term> <note>original: blicken; the "silver glance" or "flash," a sudden brightening of the metal as the last layer of oxide disappears</note>, all sorts of beautiful colors appear, like a rainbow and the like, which is a sign that it is already pure. Therefore, one must quickly cover the furnace somewhat with coals so that the metal may wash and cleanse itself all the better. Leave it standing inside for several <term>Our Fathers</term> <note>original: Vater Unser; the Lord's Prayer, used here as a common way to measure time (roughly 20-30 seconds per prayer) before precision clocks were common</note>, or until it is ready to congeal. Otherwise, if one [removes] it very soon—

->❁ (0) ❁<-

...if one wanted to remove it from the heat [too early], it tends to sputter, project outward, or as it is called, "give a mountain-cloak" <note>original: Bergmantel geben; this refers to the phenomenon of 'sprouting' or 'vegetation,' where oxygen escaping from cooling silver causes it to burst into craggy, mountain-like shapes</note>. This is otherwise considered a sign that such silver <note>original symbol: ☽</note> is poor in gold <note>original symbol: ☉</note> and does not contain much gold within it. This is therefore the shortest way to conduct <term>cupellation</term> <note>original: abtreiben; the process of refining precious metals by melting them with lead in a porous vessel</note> with profit, which is highly necessary for every alchemist to know, so that he does not fail in his operations and knows how to manage his work.

### Useful Techniques: What is Necessary to Know Regarding the Cupellation of Gold or Silver.

During cupellation, one must observe that they know how to give each batch of silver <note>original symbol: ☽</note>, or whatever the matter may be, its proper amount of lead <note>original symbol: ♄</note>. For there are sometimes all sorts of samples that are beautiful, difficult to melt, "predatory," or antimonial <note>original: ♁ialisch</note>, and often they are reluctant to "sink in" <note>referring to the base metals being absorbed into the cupel</note>.

Those samples containing silver and gold usually sink in easily, unless they happen to be mixed with a foreign additive.

Samples or silver <note>original symbol: ☽</note> containing copper require a large amount—often an eighth or a tenth part—of lead <note>original symbol: ♄</note>, through which they may be purified to their state of fineness.

Antimonial samples and the like often require a sixth part or more of lead <note>original symbol: ♄</note>; because these usually "work through" <note>meaning they are chemically aggressive or corrosive to</note> the cupels significantly, one must assist them with more lead <note>original symbol: ♄</note>.

Mercurial works otherwise sink in easily...

One must also take careful note that if the furnace should perhaps be too hot—which is indicated when one does not see the matter on the <term>cupel</term> <note>original: Capelle; a shallow, porous dish made of bone ash used to separate precious metals from base metals</note> flowing or "driving," and because of the great heat there is a mist above the cupels—then one must open the coals a little, move the cupels forward, and gradually blow with a bellows or a puffer so that the process gets moving, and the lead smoke—especially if it is an antimonial work—is driven off bit by bit, allowing the matter on the cupel to flow off all the sooner.

When it is thus in good motion or "driving," and gradually begins to produce "little flowers" <note>original: Blümlein; these are crystalline patterns of litharge (lead oxide) that appear on the surface of the molten metal</note>, which is a sign that such a sample contains gold <note>original symbol: ☉</note> or silver <note>original symbol: ☽</note>, then one must maintain and regulate the furnace and the fire at a moderate heat, so that the sample does not grow cold but rather flows toward its purity.

When the silver <note>original symbol: ☽</note> is nearly finished and is about to become pure, it immediately covers itself with a red "fire-skin"; then shortly thereafter, when it is about to <term>flash</term> <note>original: blicken; the "blick" or "brightening" is the sudden flash of light seen when the last film of oxide disappears, revealing the mirror-like surface of pure silver</note>, all kinds of beautiful colors appear, like a rainbow and the like, which is a sign that it is already pure. Therefore, one must quickly close the furnace somewhat with coals, so that it may wash and purify itself all the better, and let it stand therein for several more *Our Fathers* <note>original: Vater Unser; the time it takes to recite the "Our Father" prayer was a standard way for early modern craftsmen to measure short durations without a clock</note>, or until it is ready to solidify. ### Useful Manual Techniques: What is Necessary to Know Regarding the Cupellation of Gold or Silver.

In the process of cupellation, one must observe that they know how to give each silver <note>original symbol: ☽</note>—or whatever the matter may be—its proper amount of lead <note>original symbol: ♄; lead acts as a solvent that carries away impurities as it soaks into the porous cupel</note>. For there are sometimes all sorts of samples that are beautiful, yet are hard-flowing, "predatory" <note>original: räuberisch; a metallurgical term for ores that "rob" or consume the precious metals or the cupel itself during the melt</note>, antimonial <note>original symbol: ♁ialisch</note>, and often go into the melt reluctantly.

Those containing silver and gold usually melt easily, unless they have been mixed with a foreign additive.

Copper-bearing samples or silver <note>original symbol: ☽</note> need a great deal [of lead], often an eighth or tenth part of lead <note>original symbol: ♄</note>, by which they may be purified to their final fineness.

The antimonial <term>etc.</term> <note>original: 2c.; an archaic scribal abbreviation for "et cetera"</note> and similar materials often need a sixth part or more of lead <note>original symbol: ♄</note> because they usually work through the cupels very aggressively; thus one must assist them with some lead <note>original symbol: ♄</note>.

Mercury-based works otherwise melt easily—

When it is believed that the cupels <term>Capellen: small, porous bowls made of bone ash used to separate precious metals from lead</term> are sufficiently warmed, and the lead or the material "drives" <note>the alchemical term for the oxidation and absorption of lead into the cupel</note> upon the cupel, flowing beautifully bright and clear, then the coals are removed. Sometimes the material "stocks" <note>geſtockt: freezes or solidifies prematurely</note> upon the cupel and will not drive. It will then soon begin to drive again.

Sometimes a "glass" <note>a layer of lead oxide or slag</note> also forms upon the cupel. One must then likewise place a coal upon it and close the furnace well with coals so that it glows thoroughly. Afterward, the coals are removed from the cupel again, and it is allowed to continue running off.

It is also customary at times to apply a small amount of raw Antimony <note>original symbol: ♁</note> if the material has cooled or refuses to drive at all; this "opens" the material and very quickly gets the process moving again. This is especially useful when one has "tin-work" <note>materials with high tin content</note>, which tends to become grainy <note>original: krauppen</note> and is reluctant to melt down. In these cases, the Antimony <note>original symbol: ♁</note> or Prepared Arsenic <note>original: Arsenicus præparatus; a chemical additive used as a flux</note> eats away all the "wildness" <note>impurities or unruly chemical reactions</note> and gets the process moving. ### Another way to cupel silver without damage / and to assay it correctly.

One must fill the assaying or cupellation furnace well with coals / so that you can see the cupel / just a little bit / to observe how it proceeds or smokes / for as long as it smokes / it is a sign / that Lead <note>original symbol: ♄</note> is still present in the assay / and it is not yet pure. ### Another Way to Refine Silver Without Harm / and to Assay Correctly.

->❁ (0) ❁<-

...for [otherwise] the assays [become] smaller than usual and are false. But whoever wishes to assay correctly must, above all things, well understand the governance and order of the fire, so that the assays remain accurate and one may know how to rely upon them.

If, however, the ore-assays have finished together in this way, do not lift or remove them too soon; otherwise they will "take fright" <note>original: "erschrecken" — a technical term for when molten metal cools too rapidly, causing it to sputter, crack, or eject small particles</note> and commonly tear apart, and the assays will again be false. When they "flash" <note>original: "blicken" — the silver-glance; a momentary brightening of the metal surface indicating that all lead has been oxidized and the silver is now pure</note>, then quickly remove them from the cupel <term>cupel: a shallow, porous vessel (often made of bone ash) used in assaying to separate precious metals from lead</term> and let them cool gradually; and if anything should still hang to the bottom of the assays, wipe it away or scrape it off with a scratch-brush <term>scratch-brush: a wire brush used for cleaning metal</term>, and thus make such assays clean.

One should not scrape the assays with a knife, for they easily become false because of it, as some of the content <note>original: "Halt" — the proportion or purity of precious metal within the sample</note> is immediately scraped away.

### How one should assay all kinds of ore in the common manner; a very certain way to find what content of Gold <note>original symbol: ☉</note>, Silver <note>original symbol: ☽</note>, or other metals each contains.

Minerals and ores are most safely assayed in this manner: One takes a specimen or piece from the rock or shaft of the ores one wishes to assay, crushes them well, and sieves them fine. From these, one takes an assay-quintal <term>assay-quintal: a small standardized weight used by assayers to represent a much larger commercial hundredweight (Centner)</term>, places it in the refining furnace <term>refining furnace: an "Abtreib-Ofen," used for separating silver from lead</term> under the muffle <term>muffle: a ceramic semi-cylinder that protects the metal from direct contact with fuel while allowing heat and air to reach it</term>, and allows it to glow thoroughly. For through
