Through roasting and calcining <note>original: "Ausglühen" — heating to a high temperature without melting to remove volatile impurities</note>, all stubborn <note>original: "strenge" — refractory or difficult to process</note> ores are made docile, so that the arsenical mercury <term>mercury (☿): here likely referring to volatile spirits or arsenical vapors rather than liquid quicksilver</term> or other "wild" impurities <note>original: "Wildigkeiten" — unruly or volatile substances that interfere with smelting</note> may depart from them. Thereafter, for such a roasted assay-hundredweight <term>assay-hundredweight: a small proportional weight used in testing to represent a full-scale hundredweight</term> of ore, one takes 8 assay-hundredweights of small granulated or rasped lead <term>lead (♄)</term>, mixes them well together, and places it under the muffle <term>muffle: a clay enclosure in a furnace that protects the sample from direct flame and ash</term>. Let it scorify well, so that the impurity departs and the silver <term>silver (☽)</term> or gold <term>gold (☉)</term> which the ore carries may be absorbed into the lead <term>lead (♄)</term>. 

When it has completely melted and scorified, so that it contracts in the scorifier <term>scorifier: a shallow clay dish used in assaying</term> to about the size of a groschen <note>a small silver coin</note>, or "gets an eye" <note>a technical term for when the molten metal pools clearly in the center of the slag</note>, then one removes the scorifier, lets it cool, or pours it out. One knocks the lead-king <term>lead-king: the bead of metallic lead containing the precious metals, found at the bottom of the slag</term> out of the scorifier, if one does not wish to pour it out; this is cleaned of the slag and cupelled <note>original: "treibt... abe" — the process of oxidative melting to separate lead from precious metals</note> on a well-warmed cupel. Thus one finds what each ore contains.

If, however, such ore is infusible and reluctant to melt, one can sprinkle some of the flux-powder described below upon it and close the furnace well; or sprinkle finely crushed charcoal dust with some salt <term>salt (⊖): likely common salt or nitre</term> and mercury <term>mercury (☿)</term> upon it, and it will soon melt <note>original: "eingehen"</note> and begin to boil.

Chert <term>Hornstein: a variety of quartz or flint</term> and cobalt ores usually melt with difficulty, and if they do not melt...

### Reduction <note>original: "Reductio"</note>.

One takes the slags from the scorifier <term>scorifier: a shallow clay dish used in assaying</term> in which one suspects some metal remains, weighs them carefully, and takes—for example, if they weigh 2 lots <term>lot: a historical unit of weight, roughly 15 grams or half an ounce</term>—just as much finely crushed fast-acting flux <term>flux: a chemical agent, like potash or borax, used to lower the melting point and clean the metal</term>. Then, one adds 1 lot of finely crushed glass and 3 lots of litharge <term>litharge: lead oxide, often called "silver-glance"</term>. All of these are mixed well together and placed into a melting crucible <term>crucible: a ceramic pot built to withstand the intense heat of a furnace</term>. The "king" <term>king: a technical term for the "regulus" or the metallic bead that settles at the bottom of the mixture</term>, however, is placed at the very bottom. Cover the top of the crucible with a layer of salt so that the mixture does not rise <note>the salt acts as a stabilizer to prevent the molten material from bubbling over</note>, and cover the crucible so that no impurities fall inside. Set it in the wind-furnace <term>wind-furnace: a high-heat furnace that relies on a strong draft of air</term> and let it flow well for half an hour so that the slags are thoroughly purified. When the fire has subsided and the crucible has cooled somewhat, break it open, remove the fused "king," and place it on a well-heated scorifier. If the "king" is pure and does not contain much antimonial <note>original: "Antimonialiſches" — referring to antimony, a brittle metal that complicates the refining process</note> material, one can place it directly on the cupel <term>cupel: a small, porous bowl made of bone ash used to separate precious metals from base metals</term> and drive it off <note>original: "abtreiben" — the final stage of refining, or cupellation, where lead is oxidized and absorbed by the dish, leaving only gold or silver behind</note>:

->And let it scorify well, then place it on the cupel and refine it.<-

### Yet another faster method for assaying and processing various mountain ores or specimens in the common manner.

One takes a specimen <term>specimen: a sample piece of ore, often called a 'stufe'</term> from the mountain or rock where one suspects something of value might be found, crushes it into a powder, and then lets it calcine <term>calcine: to heat a substance to a high temperature to remove volatile impurities</term> thoroughly on a scorifier <term>scorifier: a shallow clay dish used in assaying</term> within the assay furnace. Afterward, one takes the crushed and calcined ore and weighs it according to the mining regulations on an assay scale. One takes one mining-hundredweight <term>mining-hundredweight: a specific unit of weight used in mining, proportionally scaled down for laboratory assays</term> of the roasted ore and adds 8 mining-hundredweights of finely granulated lead. These are mixed well together and allowed to melt and scorify <term>scorify: the process of turning impurities into slag using lead and heat</term> on a scorifier. When the mixture is well heated and the lead begins to "drive" <note>the 'driving' or 'treiben' of lead refers to the visible movement or oxidation on the surface of the molten metal during the assay process</note>, scatter a mixture of gold <term>gold: represented by the symbol ☉</term> and borax <term>borax: a mineral salt used as a flux to lower the melting point</term> over it. Later, when it has melted thoroughly, pour it out or let it cool in the scorifier. Strike out the king <term>king: the 'regulus' or metallic bead that settles at the bottom of the slag</term> and cupel it <term>cupel: to refine the metal bead by heating it in a bone-ash cup to remove the lead</term>; in this way, one sees the true assay and what each ore yields in the common manner.

One takes one lot <term>lot: a historical unit of weight, roughly 15 grams</term> of finely ground copper <term>copper: represented by the symbol ♀</term>, which

### Quite an easy way to assay ores reliably and quickly in the common manner.

Take an ore, no matter how low in quality it may be, and roast it very well; for through roasting, all ores become mild, and the stubborn ones become fluid, and their wildness <note>the "wildness" or "Wildigkeit" refers to impurities like sulfur, arsenic, or antimony that make the ore difficult to process or make the resulting metal brittle</note> is taken from them. Let it cool and grind it very small, then process it into a clear, pure concentrate <term>concentrate: Schlich, the fine, heavy ore mud or powder left after washing away lighter waste rock</term>. Make this dry and place one assay-hundredweight <term>assay-hundredweight: Probier-Centner, a small, proportional unit of weight used by assayers to represent a full-scale hundredweight</term> of it by weight onto the scorifier with 2 Loth of granulated lead. ## To assay silver or other coins finely.

He who wishes to finely assay Silver <note>original: ☽, the alchemical symbol for silver/the moon</note> which is of low content or mixed with copper, should laminate it or hammer it thin, make it into small sheets, and make 2 cuts <note>samples</note> according to the Mark <term>Mark: a standard unit of weight for precious metals</term>, each cut separately, and put them into folded paper. However, one must first let 3 cupels <term>cupel: a small, porous bowl made of bone ash (Capellen) used to separate precious metals from base metals</term> that are suitable for it heat through or "breathe out" <note>original: abädmen; this refers to preheating the cupels in the furnace to remove moisture and gases so they do not spit or crack during the assay</note> well in the cupellation furnace for 1 or 2 hours. Then, when they are well heated, place one sample into each of the 2 cupels. 

Once the paper has burned away, give each its proper amount of lead. In the third cupel, also put the same weight of lead for the lead grain <term>lead grain: Bleykorn; since lead often contained trace amounts of silver, a "blank" sample of pure lead was processed alongside the ore so its silver content could be subtracted from the final result</term>, which is subtracted from the assays. Close the furnace well and let it proceed with a gentle fire. Once they have finished <note>i.e., when the lead has oxidized and been absorbed into the porous cupel, leaving only a bead of pure silver behind</note>, lift them out quickly and clean them off neatly. 

Place these onto an assay balance, one sample in each pan. Lift them both at the same time and weigh them against each other so that neither is heavier than the other; if they are equal, you have assayed correctly. But if one is heavier than the other, you have not assayed correctly. If one is indeed heavier than the other, you must again make 2 cuts [samples], even if it is Silver...

In this manner, you can also finely assay Silver <note>original: ☽</note>, Copper <note>original: ♀</note>, Tin <note>original: ♃</note>, Lead <note>original: ♄</note>, Mercury <note>original: ☿</note>, etc., as well as all scrap-metal <term>Pagament: a mixture of various base and precious metals, typically from melted-down or devalued coins</term> or other currencies. If you proceed correctly in this, you will assay accurately.

### How one should recognize the assays or assay-grains, whether they contain Gold <note>original: ☉</note> or not, which happens quickly.

Take such an assay or grain and rub it onto a touchstone <term>touchstone: Probier-Stein; a dark, hard stone used to test the purity of precious metals by the color of the streak they leave when rubbed against it</term>, whether the sample be large or small. Then, dip a small quill into *Aqua fortis* <note>original: Aqu. fortis; Latin for "strong water," the historical name for nitric acid</note> and place 1 or 2 drops onto the streak. Let it act upon the mark for a little while; if it contains Gold <note>original: ☉</note>, then the streak remains or can still be seen. However, if it contains no Gold and is Silver <note>original: ☽</note>, the streak will disappear entirely.

### Yet another assay for Gold <note>original: ☉</note>

Take half a Loth <term>Loth: a traditional German unit of weight, approximately 14.6 grams or half an ounce</term> of Vitriol <note>original: ⊕; likely referring to copper or iron sulfate</note>, and 1 Loth of Sal Ammoniac <note>original: ⚹; ammonium chloride</note>. Grind them well together and moisten them with vinegar; make it thin like a light porridge. Dip a pointed quill into it and stroke it onto the touchstone where the assays were rubbed. Let it stand there for a short while, then wipe away the coloration <note>original: Colorize; referring to the chemical residue or stain</note> or what you have rubbed onto the stone. If the assay contains Gold,

### The True Basis for Refining and Assaying All Kinds of Coinage to Determine What the Fine Coin Contains.

First, one should examine every coin with great diligence to see whether it is of high quality or debased, whether it has too much copper or not, or other admixtures <term>Zusatz: alloying materials added to precious metals, often to lower their value or harden them</term>. Rub a coin known to be good onto a touchstone <term>touchstone: Gold- oder Probier-Stein; a dark, hard stone used to test the purity of precious metals by the color of the streak they leave when rubbed against it</term>, and rub the debased one that you wish to test next to it; in this way, you will soon see which is better.

Likewise: Secondly, there are several Groschen, Pfennige, or Heller <note>Groschen, Pfennige, and Heller were common German denominations of the time</note> that are very debased and poor in fineness <term>Korn: the actual content of precious metal in a coin</term>. Therefore, if you wish to know this beforehand, you must cut it open to the core <note>original: nach der Morch</note> and assay it properly.

Likewise: If there is a great quantity of coinage, one may granulate <term>körnen: the process of melting metal and pouring it into water to create small grains, which ensures a representative sample for testing</term> it all together so that it is assayed correctly. This allows one to conduct buying and selling in all trades; whatever is discovered through the assay is paid for according to its dignity and worth, whether it be gold <note>original: ☉</note>, silver <note>original: ☽</note>, copper <note>original: ♀</note>, lead <note>original: ♄</note>, or something else.

If there is only a small amount of debased coinage, one should cast it with tin, hammer it thin, or laminate it, and assay it in the same manner as one would assay copper <note>original: ♀</note>, tin <note>original: ♃</note>, or other metals.

## How one should separate or dissolve the silver in which one suspects gold might be contained; how to proceed further with it, as follows.

One takes <term>☽</term> <note>silver; represented by the alchemical symbol for the moon</note> that comes from the cupel <note>a small, porous bowl used in refining to absorb base metals</note> or is otherwise fine, and granulates it, or melts it and pours it into a casting mold as if for tin. Hammer it into thin sheets, cut small pieces from it, and place them into a small parting flask. Pour a good "precipitate" or a <term>☽</term> <note>silver</note>-saturated <term>Aqua Fortis</term> <note>nitric acid; the name means "strong water"</note> onto it—specifically, 2 <term>Loth</term> <note>a unit of weight, roughly 1/2 ounce or 15-16 grams</note> of Aqua Fortis for every 1 Loth of silver <term>☽</term>. Plug the small flask slightly, though not too tightly, so that it has a little air; otherwise, it will boil over. Gradually place it over a small bed of embers <note>original: Glüthel</note>, but not too suddenly at first. Let it work and boil a little until it has completely consumed the silver <term>☽</term> and the Aqua Fortis in the flask appears bright and clear; the silver is now held within this water.

And if such silver contains <term>☉</term> <note>gold; represented by the alchemical symbol for the sun</note>, one will see a black matter like charcoal lying at the bottom, and this is the gold <term>☉</term> which that silver contained, be it much or little, according to how rich it is. But if one sees little or no black material at the bottom, then the silver contains nothing and the separation is not worth the effort.

Then, very carefully pour off the clear water in which the silver <term>☽</term> is contained into the sugar pot <note>a deep, tapered ceramic vessel used here for collecting the silver solution</note>,

One then pours such gold <term>☉</term>-calx from the small flask into a clean little crucible / gradually pours the water <term>▽</term> away from it / dries and gradually anneals <note>heats to a high temperature</note> such gold <term>☉</term>-calx / then one will see / how high its grade is in the assay / and how much of it there is.

Regarding the silver <term>☽</term> <note>symbol for the Moon, representing silver</note>, however, which is in the water <term>▽</term> <note>symbol for water, here referring to the acid solution</note> and has been precipitated by the copper <term>♀</term> <note>symbol for Venus, representing copper</note>: one tests the water to see if any silver <term>☽</term> remains inside by sprinkling in a very small amount of salt <term>🜔</term> <note>symbol for salt or sal ammoniac</note>. If there is still silver inside, this water <term>▽</term> will form clouds, like milk, and allow more to fall to the bottom. Let it stand overnight so that it may precipitate <note>original: "præcipitire"; the process where a solid settles out of a liquid solution</note> completely. If, however, there is nothing and no silver <term>☽</term> left inside, the salt <term>🜔</term> will fall to the bottom and create no more clouds; all of this is to be observed.

When it has thus stood for a day and night, or longer as desired, pour off the clear water. Pour the silver calx <note>the fine powder of silver recovered from the solution</note> into a crucible and let it sit; the water will rise to the top, and once it is clear, it is poured away. The silver calx is then dried and absorbed into lead <term>♄</term> <note>symbol for Saturn, representing lead</note> on a refining dish <note>original: "Treib-Scherben"; a shallow ceramic dish used for heating and purifying metals</note>. Afterward, one cupels <note>original: "capelliret"; to refine metal in a "cupel," a porous pot that absorbs impurities like lead when heated</note> it to fineness, and in this way, one recovers the fine silver <term>☽</term> for use. This is the most direct way that one should perform the parting.

### How one should assay gold through inquartation and dissolve the silver <term>☽</term>.

### To Reduce the Gold.
<note>In this context, "reducing" means melting the small, porous fragments of refined gold back into a solid mass.</note>

If you wish to reduce all the little tubes <note>the "cornets" or scrolls of gold left over after the acid treatment</note> together, place them in a clean, triangular crucible <note>original: "Tiegelein"</note> with a lid, and strew just a little borax <note>original: "Borras," a salt used as a flux to lower the melting point and prevent oxidation</note> over them. Moisten it slightly with water and let it boil away; once it is dried, blow upon it cleanly <note>using a blowpipe to direct a flame and increase the heat</note> until it flows together. When it has melted and appears bright, pour it into an ingot <note>original: "Zinn"; likely "Zain," a small metal bar</note>. Then, rub it on the touchstone next to the first mark; thus you will see how much higher it has been "crowned" or graded. If you wish to have it graded to the very highest degree, pour it through antimony <term>♁</term> <note>the symbol for the Earth, here representing antimony; used as a "scavenger" to remove the last traces of base metals from gold</note>, as shall be taught hereafter; then it will have the highest purity.

### How to recover the silver—which, as previously mentioned, entered the Aqua Fortis during parting—so that it becomes silver <term>☽</term> <note>the symbol for the Moon, representing silver</note> once again.

In this way, you will obtain fine silver <term>☽</term> as before. Such silver <term>☽</term> is much better for parting <note>original: "quartiren," the process of alloying gold with silver to then separate them with acid</note> and similar tasks than the other kind. This is the complete foundation of parting <note>original: "Scheiden," the general term for the chemical separation of metals</note> and such operations.

### How to refine Gold / and cast it through Antimony.

Take the <term>Gold</term> <note>original symbol: ☉</note> that you wish to make fine / and with it three times as much weight of <term>Antimony</term> <note>original: *Spießglas*, referring to stibnite or antimony sulfide</note> / which is freshly liquid and has never before been used in any work / place the Antimony in a <term>crucible</term> <note>original: *Tiegel*; a ceramic pot capable of withstanding extreme heat</note> in the fire / let it melt / and cast the Gold into it piece by piece / and not all at once. When it then gives off a white smoke rising upwards / and the flames of the fire turn white and yellow mixed together / then pour the Antimony along with the Gold into a <term>casting cone</term> <note>original: *Gießbuckel*; a conical iron mold used to separate metals by density</note> / which has been previously greased with <term>tallow</term> <note>original: *Unschlit*; animal fat used to prevent the metal from sticking to the mold</note> / and tap steadily on the casting cone with a small hammer or a <term>striking-bone</term> <note>original: *Schlagebein*; a heavy bone or tool used to vibrate the mold</note> / or shake it well back and forth / until it solidifies.

In this way, the Gold settles as a <term>Regulus</term> <note>original: *König* (King); the heavy, purified metallic button found at the bottom of the mold</note> at the base of the casting cone; when it has cooled / turn the casting cone upside down over a block / and strike the bottom of the cone / so that the Antimony falls out / and at the very bottom you will find the Gold-Regulus. Put the remaining Antimony back in / and melt it by itself / so the <term>Antimony</term> <note>original symbol: ♁</note> deposits another Regulus; break this off / and keep it with the first one. Likewise, take the Antimony for a third time / melt it again by itself / pour it into the casting cone / and do just as before / and it will deposit a third Regulus / break that off. ## How one should make touchneedles for testing silver.

These needles are commonly graded in 16 steps, from one <term>Loth</term> <note>a historical unit of weight, approximately 1/16 of a Mark or about 14-17 grams</note> up to one <term>Mark</term> <note>a unit of weight equal to 16 Loth</note>. Although one might increase by half-Loth increments / which would give 32 needles / each of these needles would not be easily distinguished from the others; therefore, it is better to stay with the 16 needles.

These needles or pins are cast from copper / and the mixture of the metals or samples at the bottom for the first needle is as follows: Take 1 part fine <term>cupelled silver</term> <note>original: *Capellen-☽*; silver that has been purified in a cupel to remove all base metals</note> / and 15 parts pure <term>copper</term> <note>original symbol: ♀</note> / melt them together / and solder or cast it onto the bottom of the needle. To the second needle take 2 parts fine <term>silver</term> <note>original symbol: ☽</note> / and 14 parts copper. To the third needle 3 parts <term>silver</term> <note>original symbol: ☽</note> and 13 parts <term>copper</term> <note>original symbol: ♀</note>. To the 4th needle 4 parts fine <term>silver</term> <note>original symbol: ☽</note> / and 12 parts <term>copper</term> <note>original symbol: ♀</note>. ## How the fast-flowing flux is made, which is very useful to use for reducing and other tasks that do not easily succeed.

One takes equal parts <note>original: *ana*; a medical and alchemical term meaning "of each"</note> of tartar <note>potassium bitartrate, a sediment from wine barrels</note> and saltpeter, crushes them together finely, puts half of it into a mortar or eventually into a crucible, and throws a red-hot coal into it, so that it begins to burn. The remainder is thrown in bit by bit; stir it up from the bottom with an iron rod and let it completely deflagrate <note>original: *verpuffen*; to burn away rapidly with a small explosion or hiss</note> until it becomes a white stone or salt. This must be removed quickly before the mortar cools, as it flows easily <note>meaning it is prone to absorbing moisture or melting</note>. This is used for the reduction of metals <note>restoring a metal from its oxide or ore to its metallic state</note> and other things. However, if it is left exposed to the air, it soon flows into an "oil" <note>a concentrated liquid formed when a salt absorbs atmospheric moisture</note>; this oil fixes <term>precipitated mercury</term> <gloss>original: ☿ Mercurium præcipitatum</gloss> and similar sulfurs <note>alchemical term for flammable or oily substances</note>. If you wish to keep such a flux so that it does not liquefy, seal it in a glass or keep it constantly in a lukewarm place; it is useful for very many things.

Then pour this into a glazed, red-hot pot, and with a

### On Cementation.

#### How Gold <gloss>original symbol: ☉</gloss> and Silver <gloss>original symbol: ☽</gloss> can be brought to their proper state through cementation.

It should be known that cementation is a particularly useful art, through which one can pickle and etch away copper, brass, silver, and other metals from gold using a moistened powder, so that the gold <gloss>original symbol: ☉</gloss> is separated from its additives and remains unharmed. However, such cementation is only to be used on such coinage as is more than half gold <gloss>original symbol: ☉</gloss>; for where the silver <gloss>original symbol: ☽</gloss> is more than the gold <gloss>original symbol: ☉</gloss>, it is better to use <term>parting</term> <note>a chemical process, usually involving nitric acid, to separate gold from silver</note>, as it proceeds more quickly, in less time, and with less effort and expense. It must be known, however, that the cements must be prepared according to whether the gold is poor or rich <note>meaning whether the gold content is low or high</note>.

The materials and substances required for the cement-powder are those which [affect] the silver and copper...

Otherwise, these impurities would mostly remain lying and hanging on the gold. Through this, the gold becomes pure and clean.

### How the **Aqua Fortis** <term>Aq. fort.: "strong water" or nitric acid, the primary chemical used to separate gold from silver</term> should be purified and precipitated from its **dregs** <note>original: "Fæcibus"</note>, so that during the parting process it does not drop any impurities, ensuring the gold remains pure and is not contaminated by silver.

In this matter, it must be considered that if the **silver** <gloss>original symbol: ☽</gloss> is put into such Aqua Fortis—which has only just been distilled and is still raw—to **dissolve** <note>original: "solviren"</note> it, everything becomes mixed together and is not good for parting, which is why one cannot obtain a proper assay <insert>...to hold</insert> <note>catchword: "halten"</note>

[able to] hold [impurities]; therefore, one should clean and precipitate it from its **dregs** <note>original: "Fæcibus"</note>, so that it becomes clear and suitable for **parting** <term>Scheiden: the alchemical process of separating gold from silver using acid</term>. This is done as follows:

If there is 1 **pound** <note>original: "lbj."</note> of distilled **Aqua Fortis** <term>Aq. fort.: "strong water" or nitric acid</term>, one pours [a bit] of such Aqua Fortis into a small **parting flask** <note>original: "Scheide-Kölblein"</note> and dissolves 1 **quentlein** <term>Quentlein: a historical German unit of weight, roughly 3.6 grams</term> of pure **silver** <gloss>original symbol: ☽</gloss> in it. Because this is still warm and dissolved, one pours this dissolved silver into the newly distilled Aqua Fortis, which immediately becomes white and cloudy like milk. Shake this together 2 or 3 times during the day and let it stand in a lukewarm place day and night. In this way, the impurity mostly all settles to the bottom, like a white lime or chalk; when it has become clear, pour it off into a clean glass and keep it for parting, for it is a pure and precipitated Aqua Fortis.

**Wash until sweet** <term>süsse aus: the process of rinsing a substance with water to remove all traces of acid or salts until the water no longer tastes "sour" or sharp</term> the dregs or what remains of the **silver** <gloss>original symbol: ☽</gloss> from the Aqua Fortis, and dry them. Sometimes, if one has no fine silver at hand, one is accustomed to precipitate the distilled Aqua Fortis with other **coinage** <note>original: "Müntze"</note>, which is just as good for parting as if it were precipitated with cupelled silver. However, such Aqua Fortis does not remain so white and bright; instead, because there is still some **copper** <note>original: "Kupffer"</note> in that same coinage, the **parting-water** <gloss>original symbol: ∇</gloss> becomes greenish from it. Those [acids] precipitated with **coppery coinage** <note>Low-purity coins containing high amounts of copper</note> are not suitable for **gold** <gloss>original symbol: ☉</gloss> assays at all, because the **copper** <gloss>original symbol: ♀</gloss> found in such coins and in the **Aqua Fortis** <term>nitric acid</term> clings to the **gold calx** <term>the fine gold powder remaining after silver is dissolved away</term> just as it does to **silver** <gloss>original symbol: ☽</gloss>. It is also difficult to **wash until sweet** <term>the process of rinsing a substance with water until all traces of acid or salts are removed</term> from it, which would then be harmful in a pure assay. 

If, however, after the first precipitation the Aqua Fortis is still somewhat impure and does not become clear, one must precipitate it again with a small amount of **silver** <gloss>original symbol: ☽</gloss> until it becomes pure. Then it is sufficient and can be used very effectively for the **parting** <term>separating gold from silver</term> of silver and other gold-bearing samples, or for whatever work one has.

### How to strengthen weak Aqua Fortis that does not "attack" or wish to dissolve the gold, and how to remedy such a situation.

It happens sometimes that during the **distillation** <note>original: "Scheide-∇ Brennen," literally "parting-fire burning"</note> a mistake is made: the joints were not well **luted** <term>sealed with a specialized clay or paste to make the apparatus airtight</term>, the **spirits** <term>the volatile acidic vapors</term> escaped, the water remained too weak, and during the parting process such water will not "attack" the **silver** <gloss>original symbol: ☽</gloss>. 

Therefore, one can strengthen such water in two ways: First, one prepares a mixture of **vitriol** <gloss>original symbol: 🜔</gloss> and **calcined** <term>heated until moisture is driven off and it becomes a dry powder</term> **alum** <gloss>original symbol: 🜫</gloss> and distills it in the usual manner to produce Aqua Fortis. Before beginning, however, one pours the weak Aqua Fortis into the **receiver** <term>the glass vessel, or "Vorlage," that collects the distilled liquid</term> and then performs the distillation of the mixture. Finally, one drives the spirits over thoroughly; thus the spirits pass into the [weak] water, and it strengthens the...

...suggested **Aqua Fortis** <term>nitric acid</term>, so that it is very good and can be excellently used for **parting** <term>the process of separating gold from silver</term>. The other preparation is that one puts the weak **Aqua Fortis** into a **flask** <note>original: "Kolben," a cucurbit or boiling flask</note> and draws the wateriness away from it until the **spirits** <term>the concentrated acidic vapors</term> arrive—which is noticed by the brownish color <note>this refers to the nitrogen dioxide gas produced as the acid concentrates</note>. Then, what remains in the flask is once again strong **Aqua Fortis**. One keeps the **phlegm** <term>the watery, low-acid distillate collected at the start</term> to add to the receiver <note>original: "Vorschlagen"</note> if one wishes to distill <note>original: "brennen," literally "to burn"</note> more new **Aqua Fortis**.

### How one should quickly distill a good Aqua Fortis in 5 or 6 hours.

Take for the ingredients 3 lbs. of **vitriol** <gloss>original symbol: ⦶</gloss> and 3 lbs. of **calcined alum** <gloss>original symbol: 🜔; alum heated to remove its water content</gloss>, both finely crushed, and mix in 5 or 6 lbs. of finely crushed **quicklime** <term>unslaked lime</term>. These pieces, well mixed, are placed into a **retort** <term>a glass or ceramic vessel with a long, downward-pointing neck</term>, and in the large **receiver** <term>the vessel that collects the distillate</term>, a little **Aqua Fortis** is added beforehand. 

When everything is **sealed with lute** <term>verlutirt—the joints are made airtight with a specialized clay or paste</term> and has become well dried, then one builds a fire beneath it and lets the liquid pass over quickly, in such a way that the water and **spirits** <term>acidic vapors</term> always come over together. Because the ingredients are mixed with the **lime**, one does not have to worry about the mixture boiling over <note>the lime acts as a buffering agent to prevent sudden surges or foaming</note>. Increase the fire from hour to hour until the water and spirits have come over; finally, the jar or **retort** must glow <note>heated to a red glow to ensure all acid is driven out of the mineral salts</note>. In this manner and work, one can distill a good **parting-water** <term>Aqua Fortis</term> in 5 or 6 hours, which otherwise requires a day and a night, or 24 hours.

### To make a good Aqua Regia.

Take 1 lb. <note>original: "lbj."</note> of common **Aqua Fortis** <term>nitric acid</term>. Pour it over 8 **Loth** <note>a unit of weight; 8 Loth is approximately 4 ounces</note> or 1/2 lb. <note>original: "lbß."</note> of **salmiac** <gloss>original symbol: ⚹; ammonium chloride</gloss>, which has been crushed very fine, according to how strong you want it to be. Then, distill the **Aqua Fortis** off from the salmiac once more; thus you have a good **Aqua Regia** <note>original: "Aqu. Regis," literally "royal water," an acidic mixture capable of dissolving gold</note>.

## To grain silver or lead finely,
->and to granulate them.<-

Silver or lead is indeed often grained by pouring it through a broom into cold water; however, it is better that you take two unglazed earthen pots that you can easily and securely fit or invert over one another. Coat the insides with **tallow** <term>Unschlit; rendered animal fat</term> or wax, and sprinkle plenty of **alum** <gloss>original symbol: 🜔</gloss> inside. Then, melt the silver or lead. Pour it quickly into one pot and immediately invert the other over it, and shake it back and forth, up and down, with both hands until you believe it has solidified. Then, lift off the top pot, and you will find the **silver** <gloss>original symbol: ☽</gloss> finely grained, like **poppy seeds** <note>original: "Magsaamen"</note> or small buckshot. If any pieces are larger, put them back in, melt them, and do as before until it is all granulated finely enough to your liking.

# An excellent wind-furnace, convenient for use in all works an alchemist intends, and by which many other frustrations can be avoided.

### The Preparation of the Furnace.

This following wind-furnace, which Glauber shares in his *Furnaces* <note>original: "Furnis," referring to Johann Rudolf Glauber’s influential work "Furni Novi Philosophici" (New Philosophical Furnaces), published in the mid-17th century</note>, is invaluable because of its uncommon utility and because one is spared much labor and frustration; anyone intending to work on the above experiments will be able to make good use of it.

The furnace can be made as large or as small as one wishes, depending on whether one wants to melt much or little at once, or whether one needs it large or small for their work. If the furnace is one **foot** <term>Werck-Schuh; literally a "work-shoe," a historical unit of measurement roughly equivalent to a modern foot</term> wide on the inside, one can place a **crucible** <gloss>a ceramic or metal container in which metals may be melted</gloss> in it that holds approximately 4, 5, or 6 **marks** <term>Marck; a unit of weight for precious metals, usually about 8 ounces or 230 grams</term>. However, if one wished to use larger crucibles, the furnace would also have to be larger.

And it must be constructed in a square shape from good fire-resistant stones and **clay** <term>Luto; from the Latin "lutum," referring to a heat-resistant sealing clay or "lute" used to build and seal furnaces</term>, rising 1 or 2 feet from the ground before placing the grate. This grate must be made in such a way that if it becomes [clogged] with overflow from the crucibles, **slag** <term>Schlacken; the stony waste matter separated from metals during smelting</term>, or other refuse that has come in with the coals—

below, the <term>air-intake</term> <note>original: "Windfang"</note> should be as long as the tall pipes above are required to be.

And if one has a suitable room in which, next to its chimney, another rises from below, then the <term>furnace</term> <note>original: "Ofen"</note> can be placed under the upper chimney. By breaking a hole through the wall and fitting a <term>register</term> <note>original: "Register"; a sliding damper or plate used to regulate the flow of air</note> there, the fire is forced to draw its air from the depths of the adjacent chimney. 

In this way, no special air-intake is required. One only needs to open a door or window in the room below so that the air may enter the chimney; the fire then draws the wind from the side-chimney so powerfully that it is truly a marvel to behold. No <term>bellows</term> <note>original: "Blasebalg"</note> can equal its force—so much so that if the furnace were not constructed from fire-resistant earth, it would melt away. Even the best <term>crucibles</term> <note>original: "Tiegel"</note>, when standing for a long time in such a great fire, often cannot endure it, but rather collapse and melt. For this reason, the register is installed so that the fire may be governed, allowing one to provide as much or as little heat as one desires. Now follows the illustration of the furnace.

# To Weld Glass.

This is a useful little technique / and it is done as follows: One takes isinglass <note>original: "Hausenblasen," a high-quality collagen glue made from the swim bladders of fish</note> / (a gum thus named /) dissolves it in water <note>original: "Aqua"; the symbol for water is used here</note>, in such a way / that it becomes like a thin dough / with this one smears the crack of the shattered glass using a small quill / and holds it over a glowing ember / letting it dry well / so it pulls together strongly / and becomes whole again.

### The Hermetic Seal.

<note>original: "Sigillum Hermetis," an airtight seal essential for alchemical processes to prevent the escape of vapors.</note>

Tin <note>represented by the symbol ♃</note>. 5 parts Venetian Glass / finely ground amber <note>original: "Agtstein"</note> 2 parts / Borax 1 part / mix, and grind well together / moistened with water <note>original: "Aqua"; the symbol for water is used here</note> like a dough / smeared two fingers thick around the stopper / and then melted shut / which can be done with a blowpipe <note>original: "Löth-Rohr"</note> / such as goldsmiths use for soldering / but it must be somewhat longer / one takes a charcoal fire / and lays the blowpipe in the middle of the coals / so that it glows / and at one end one holds the glass / that one wishes to melt shut / and which has been smeared / as mentioned / one blows into the other side of the pipe / so the heat is concentrated onto the glass / and makes it flow with the applied flux <note>original: "Fluß," a substance used to lower the melting point of the glass mixture</note>: In this way, the glasses are best melted shut / which must be done / if one wishes to keep volatile spirits <note>original: "flüchtige Geister," substances that easily evaporate or turn to gas</note> in the fire for a long time / or wishes to thicken <term>coagulate</term> something.

<summary>This page describes practical methods for repairing cracked glassware using isinglass and creating a "Hermetic Seal" using a specialized glass-and-borax flux melted with a blowpipe.</summary>

# Useful Observations and Interpretations of the most prominent words and things / that often occur in Chemistry / and are used / and are highly necessary to know.

### 1.
### What Assaying is?

Assaying is / when one takes all kinds of metal-bearing ores, <term>Gold</term> <note>original symbol: ☉</note>, <term>Silver</term> <note>original symbol: ☽</note>, <term>Copper</term> <note>original symbol: ♀</note>, or other such metals, as well as coinage / wherein one suspects / that such contains <term>Gold</term> or <term>Silver</term> / and intends to purify it from all foreign additions and bring it to purity through melting / imbibing <note>original: "Einseugern," a process where molten lead "soaks up" precious metals from the ore</note> / or scorification <note>original: "Verschlacken," the process of turning impurities into glassy slag</note> and cupellation <note>original: "Abtreiben," refining gold or silver by oxidizing lead in a shallow porous dish</note>: so that one may obtain reliable samples and determine the metal content. To Assaying belong special furnaces / muffles <note>D-shaped ceramic covers that protect the sample from direct flame while allowing air to circulate</note> / hearth-plates / scorifiers / roasting grates / cupels <note>original: "Capellen," small porous bowls made of bone ash used to separate precious metals from lead</note> / and other small earthenware / belonging to the craft of assaying; Likewise good lead and crucibles.

### 2.
### Melting.

This is / when one wishes to place something into the wind-furnace / or before the bellows in the crucibles / to flow and melt / be it either <term>Gold</term> or <term>Silver</term> / which is beautifully refined for granulating / for pouring into ingots <note>original: "Zinn," likely a variant of "Zain" (ingot or metal bar), though it can also mean Tin</note> / or for other uses / whatever they may be.

If one also has metal-bearing slag / or similar material, where one suspects / still [something]...

### 3.
### Scorification.

**Scorification** <note>original: "Verschlacken," the process of turning impurities into glassy slag</note> is / precisely when one has impure <term>Gold</term> <note>original symbol: ☉</note> or <term>Silver</term> <note>original symbol: ☽</note> / that still contains <term>Copper</term> <note>original symbol: ♀</note> / <term>Lead</term> <note>original symbol: ♄</note> / or other foreign metallic additions / and one treats it with the proper amount of lead on the **scorifier** / under the **muffle** <note>a D-shaped ceramic cover that protects the sample from direct flame while allowing air to circulate</note> / with heat / or boils it down / so that it runs cleaner and sooner upon the roasting-grate or cupels / and does not crack them; especially those materials that are **antimonial** / or the like.

For scorification, one needs good scorifiers / a good flux for boiling down / lead or **litharge** <term>Glette</term> <note>lead oxide formed during the refining process</note> / each according to the nature of the matter.

### 7.
### Edulcoration or Sweetening.
<note>Edulcoration (Absüssen) is the chemical process of washing a solid precipitate to remove all traces of acid or soluble salts.</note>

For sweetening, good river- or rain-water is required / then clean vessels.

### 8.

This must be beaten thin / and cut into small pieces / so that they can fit into the parting-flasks <note>small, long-necked glass vessels used for separating gold from silver</note>.

For laminating, a hammer, a small anvil, and a pair of shears are needed / to cut the beaten sheets <note>original: "Laminen"</note> into small pieces. Granulating...

### 9.
### Blowing-off or Smoking-off.

This is/ when one prepares from the cupel <note>original: "Treib-Scherben"; a shallow, porous ceramic dish used in refining</note>, and a muffle <note>original: "Muffel"; a clay chamber used to protect materials from direct flame while allowing heat and air to circulate</note>/ or in the crucible/ **gold** <term>☉</term> <note>original symbol: ☉</note> or **silver** <term>☽</term> <note>original symbol: ☽</note>/ which has been prepared according to the art and mixed with **lead** <term>♄</term> <note>original symbol: ♄</note>/ **antimony** <term>♁</term> <note>original symbol: ♁</note>/ or other ingredients, salts and the like/ and with a bellows drives away the poisonous wildness <note>original: "gifftige Wildigkeit"; referring to volatile impurities or noxious fumes</note> and brittle salts through smoking and blowing/ so that it becomes pure and fit for other purposes.

To Blowing-off belongs a bellows/ poker/ cupels/ lead/ or whatever is necessary.

### 10.
### Amalgamation.

Amalgamation is/ when one wishes to unite and incorporate **gold** <term>☉</term> or **silver** <term>☽</term>/ or other metals, with **mercury** <term>☿</term> <note>original symbol: ☿; quicksilver</note> for the art: As when one takes 1 part **gold** <term>☉</term> or **silver** <term>☽</term> to 3 or 4 parts **mercury** <term>☿</term>/ this/ when it is mixed with warm **gold** <term>☉</term> or **silver** <term>☽</term>/ as also with warm **mercury** <term>☿</term>/ and stirred together/ then the **mercury** <term>☿</term> takes or eats the **gold** <term>☉</term> or **silver** <term>☽</term> into itself/ and it becomes a thin mass, or paste <note>original: "Mus"</note> from it/ which is called an amalgam: This is washed/ dried/ and pressed through leather/ as is customary.

->11.<-
### Calcination or Destruction.

This is when metals, ores, minerals, or similar substances are burned and destroyed in the fire—using salts, sulfurs, or other materials—to become like ashes, powder, or earth. These can afterwards be applied to many things in the Art <note>The "Art" refers to Alchemy or the "Hermetic Art"</note>. For this purpose, crucibles, pots, sulfur, salt, etc., and whatever is necessary for the art are required.

---

->12.<-
### Reverberation.

Reverberation is not very different from calcination. In this process, by means of a specific **fire** <term>fire</term> <note>original symbol: △</note>, the innermost essence of the metal, mineral, or other substance is unlocked and brought forth. With **fire** and salts, it is destroyed into an incredibly fine <note>original: "unbegreifflichen"; literally "incomprehensible," referring to a powder so fine it defies touch or sight</note> powder, and its wildness <note>original: "Wildigkeit"; a term used for volatile or brittle impurities</note> is burned away. This occurs either in a furnace or in a sealed fire.

For reverberation, as previously mentioned, there belong crucibles, pots, **antimony**, **salt**, **saltpeter** <term>antimony, salt, saltpeter</term> <note>original symbols: ♁, ⊖, 🜔; saltpeter is also known as nitrum</note>, etc., or whatever else is required for it.

---

->13.<-
### Regulating, or Casting the Regulus.

This occurs when one melts materials together and pours them into a casting-cone <note>original: "Gießbuckel"; a conical mold used to separate purified metal from slag</note>. Thus the metal falls,

This is produced from **gold**, **silver**, **antimony**, **copper** <term>gold, silver, antimony, copper</term> <note>original symbols: ☉, ☽, ♁, ♀</note>, or other containing and mixed minerals, using certain salts or flux so that the yield, or whatever else remains in the slag, is extracted. For making a regulus, one requires high-quality crucibles, a casting-cone, saltpeter, tartar, etc., and whatever else is needed for it.

### 14.
### Cementation.

Cementation is a particularly fine art by which one can etch and separate **silver**, **copper** <term>silver, copper</term> <note>original symbols: ☽, ♀</note>, brass, and other materials from **gold** <note>original: ☉</note> using moistened powders. This ensures the gold is separated from its additions and impurities <note>original: "Zusatz und Aussatz"</note> and remains unharmed. Alternatively, through this art, **silver** <note>original: ☽</note> is treated in such a way that a rich, gold-bearing separation results.

### 15.
### Digestion.

To put into **digestion** <term>digestion</term>, or to place something in heat for maturation <note>original: "Auszeitigen"; the alchemical concept of slow heating to allow a substance to "ripen" or reach perfection</note>, is when one places **gold**, **silver**, **mercury** <term>gold, silver, mercury</term> <note>original symbols: ☉, ☽, ☿</note>, or other medicinal substances, with certain oils, strong spirits, and waters, for boiling and unlocking <note>original: "auffschliessen"; a term meaning to chemically open a substance to release its inner virtues or essences</note> in sand, ash, a water bath <note>original: "Balneo"</note>, or gentle heat, in convenient places

### 16.
### Distillation.

Distillation <term>distillation</term> is a beautiful process, and nearly one of the most useful, which can hardly be dispensed with in both medicinal and mineral operations. This occurs when one drives certain waters, spirits <note>original: "Spiritus"</note>, oils <note>original: "Olea"</note>, or the like over into jugs, flasks, glasses, or retorts <term>retort</term> <note>A glass bulb with a long, downward-pointing neck used for distilling substances</note> with its appropriate fire, and extracts its best essence, which is then to be used in both the art <note>The "art" here refers specifically to alchemy</note> and in medicine.

For distillation, one requires good glass or earthenware vessels, flasks, alembics <term>alembic</term> <note>A cap or hood placed over a boiling vessel to collect and direct vapors</note>, retorts, and other receiving glasses, etc.

### 17.
### Sublimation.

This is when one—either with a specific additive or by itself <note>original: "per se"</note>—extracts from minerals or other materials its **sulfur** <term>sulfur</term> <note>original: 🜍 / "Sulphur / Schwefel"</note>, flowers <note>original: "Flores"; the fine powder or "bloom" that forms when a substance is heated and then condenses</note>, **salt** <term>salt</term> <note>original: 🜔 / "Sal / Salz"</note>, or its subtle innermost essence and most noble soul through exaltation <term>exaltation</term> <note>original: "per exaltationem"; a process of raising a substance to a higher, more purified or potent state</note> in earthenware flasks,

### 18.
### Cohibition or Repetition.

This occurs when one repeats a process that has already been prepared and completed once before, starting it anew with its specific ingredients just as in the previous work. This is done several times, as many procedures specify: for instance, when a sublimated substance is sublimated 2 or 3 times, or other things are repeated 2, 3, or more times in cementation <term>cementation</term> <note>a process of heating a solid in a powder to change its properties without melting it</note>, and so forth.

### 19.
### Deflagration.

Deflagration <term>deflagration</term> <note>original: "Verbuffen"; a rapid, "puffing" combustion or explosion used to purify or react substances</note> is when, according to the preference or the work of the operator <note>original: "operantis"</note>, one puts certain salts <note>original: "Salien"</note>, sulfurs <note>original: "Sulphura"</note>, saltpeter <term>saltpeter</term> <note>potassium nitrate, a powerful oxidizer</note>, tartar <term>tartar</term> <note>original: "Weinstein"; a salt used in various chemical reactions</note>, or similar ingredients—either for the Art <note>referring to alchemy</note>, medicine, or other purposes—with a certain mixed additive into crucibles, pots, or mortars. These are then ignited with coal dust, glowing coals, or glowing iron, and thus allowed to burn away. Likewise, one also makes...

### 20.
### Luting.

Luting <term>luting</term> <note>original: "Lutiren," from the Latin "lutum" meaning mud or clay; the process of sealing joints or coating vessels to protect them from heat</note> is when one uses clay or another *lute*, such as paste, glue, and the like, to coat the glasses that are to be placed in the sand or fire for distillation or other tasks—either with clay so that they do not crack, or with a specially made paste—and seals them <note>original: "verlutiret"</note> so that during distillation, digestion, sublimation, **etc.**, the spirits <note>volatile vapors</note> do not evaporate away.

For luting, one requires a good clay that is not stony, chaff <note>original: "spreuer"</note>, a *caput mortuum* <term>caput mortuum</term> <note>literally "dead head"; the solid residue remaining after a substance has been distilled or heated to exhaustion</note>, chopped straw <note>original: "Sperwel"</note>, coarse flour, carpenter's glue, old tar, and similar materials for coating and pasting.

### 21.
### Quartation.

Quartation <term>quartation</term> <note>original: "Quartiren"; a process of refining gold by alloying it with silver so that the silver can be dissolved away by acid</note> is when one takes gold-containing silver <note>indicated by the symbols for the Sun (gold) ☉ and the Moon (silver) ☽</note> which is so rich in gold that the *aqua fortis* <term>aqua fortis</term> <note>nitric acid; used here to dissolve silver to separate it from gold</note> cannot attack it or allow it to be separated. To such a mixture, one adds 3 or 4 times as much other cupelled silver <note>refined silver purified in a cupel</note>, laminates it <note>pounded into thin sheets to increase surface area</note>, and separates it; in this way, one finds from the very bottom what amount of the finest gold it contains and holds within it.

For quartation <term>quartation</term> <note>the process of alloying gold with silver to later separate them with acid</note>, one needs good cupel-silver <term>cupel-silver</term> <note>original: "Capellen-Silber"; silver refined on a cupel to ensure it is free of base metals</note>, clean crucibles, an ingot mold <note>original: "Inguß"</note> for pouring, granulation <note>pouring molten metal into water to form small beads</note> or lamination <note>rolling metal into thin sheets</note>, **etc.**

### 22.
### Evaporation / Coagulation.

Coagulation <term>coagulation</term> <note>original: "Coaguliren"</note> is when one brings a substance into a solid mass, or allows it to flow and boil down into a stone, a salt, or a flux; this applies to lyes, water <note>indicated by the symbol $\nabla$</note>, oils <note>original: "Olea"</note>, **etc.**

For coagulation, one requires glazed pots, clean iron pans, and whatever else is necessary for the task.

### 23.
### Filtration.

Filtration or clarification <term>clarification</term> <note>original: "Clarificiren"</note> is when one pours boiled-down water <note>symbol: $\nabla$</note>, lyes, spirits <note>volatile distillates</note>, oils, and the like—which are still impure and not clear—through a filter paper, felt, a funnel, or a filter <note>original: "Filtrum"</note> and lets it "smoke" <note>likely referring to the process of letting the liquid drain or the vapors clear</note>, whereby its sharp strength, salt, and purity pass through, and the cloudy and thick matter remains behind, separating from the clear liquid; this clear liquid is afterwards boiled down or coagulated, and such lyes may also be kept for other uses.

For filtration, one requires a filter, or a glazed funnel, glazed pots, and filter paper: likewise, what else is necessary, **etc.**

### Reduction.

### 25.
### Extraction /

This is when one draws out and unlocks the soul <note>original: "Animam"</note>, sulfur, essence <note>original: "Ens"</note>, or tincture <term>tincture</term> <note>the essential spirit or concentrated medicinal/alchemical quality of a substance</note> from minerals or materials, through the art and knowledge of the practitioner, using certain spirits, oils <note>original: "Olitäten"</note>, or acids <note>original: "scharffen Wassern"</note>, pulling these from the innermost core for either medicine or the Art, so that they may be used for the operator's purpose <note>original: "des Operantis Intent"</note>.

For **extraction**, one requires good flasks <note>original: "Kolben"</note> and similar glassware, acids, spirits, and whatever is necessary.

...if they were to merge; one should soak these into lead upon a cupel <term>cupel</term> <note>original: "Treib-Scherben"; a shallow, porous dish used in assaying</note>, allow them to slag off and flow into a regulus <term>regulus</term> <note>original: "Könige"; literally "king," referring to the metal button at the bottom of the vessel</note>, in which the content of gold <note>symbol: ☉</note> and silver <note>symbol: ☾</note> is subsequently held; this is then refined by cupellation <term>cupellation</term> <note>original: "abgetrieben"</note> to see how much gold and silver each contains.

### 27.
### Inspissation or Boiling Down.

This work is not much different from coagulation, in which one allows various prepared lyes, waters, oils <note>original: "Olitäten"</note>, and similar substances to boil, reduce, or evaporate <note>original: "abrauchen"</note> in such a way that one can obtain from them a salt <note>symbol: ⊖</note>, antimony <note>symbol: ♁</note>, or whatever else is prepared through the Art.

### 28.
### Crystallization.

To allow to **crystallize** <note>original: "anschiessen lassen"; literally "to let shoot"</note> is when acids, lyes, salts <note>original: "Salien"</note>, spirits, oils, and the like are boiled down to half their volume or somewhat more, drawn off, or evaporated, and then placed in a cold location. Crystals then immediately "shoot" or form in the shape of a white, angular, and oblong transparent salt. This is the innermost and purest essence <term>essence</term> <note>original: "Ens"; the fundamental being or spiritual core of a substance</note> or being of the substance, to be used for a tincture <note>original: "ad Tincturam"</note> and medicine <note>original: "ad Medicinam"</note>.

For **crystallization** <note>original: "Anschiessen"</note>, one requires good white sugar-glasses, bowls, or detached flasks.

### 29.
### Imbibition or Moistening.

**Imbibition** <term>imbibition</term> <note>original: "Imbibiren"; from the Latin *imbibere*, meaning "to drink in"</note> or moistening is when one pours certain powders, salts <note>original: "Salien"</note>, sulfurs <note>original: "Sulphura"</note>, or other ingredients—such as certain oils <note>original: "Olea"</note>, spirit-waters, or the like—over a substance, allowing it to dry out again several times, and soaking it as often as necessary so that the materials become fixed <term>fixed</term> <note>chemically stable and resistant to heat</note> and mild for the Art <note>the "Art" refers to the practice of alchemy or chemistry</note>.

For imbibition, one requires oils, spirits, fixing-waters, or other sharp waters <term>sharp waters</term> <note>original: "scharffe Wasser"; typically corrosive acids or solvents like aqua fortis</note>, or whatever else is desired, etc.

With this, dear reader, I conclude the present treatise <note>original: "Tractat"</note> with these practical operations: perform your work under Divine Blessing in the fear of the Lord; and should you still find yourself lacking in some of these operations, I will gladly provide further assistance in writing, as far as I am able to convey through correspondence. Farewell! I commend you to the perceptible love of God, revealed in Jesus Christ.

->* * *<-

### Corollaries.

1. It should be known that the **Regulus of Venus** <term>Regulus of Venus</term> <note>original: "♁Veneris"; a brittle metallic alloy of antimony and copper</note>, when prepared with "living" mercury as taught above **through sublimation** <note>original: "per sublimationem"; the process of heating a substance into vapor and then solidifying it again</note>, is the best material for the **exaltation** <term>exaltation</term> <note>a process to refine or increase the purity and potency of a metal</note> of gold. Otherwise, this process is difficult to achieve with other substances. Additionally, **gold** <note>represented by the symbol ☉</note> can be turned into **calx** <term>calx</term> <note>original: "Kalck"; a metal that has been reduced to a powder through heat or chemical reaction</note> by dissolving and distilling it, which can be used in place of metal filings.

2. The **Regulus of Venus** can be multiplied using **Oil of Vitriol** <term>oil of vitriol</term> <note>original: "Oleum Vitrioli"; concentrated sulfuric acid</note> as follows: dissolve 1 part of the separated Regulus in **aqua fortis** <term>aqua fortis</term> <note>nitric acid</note>, pour in 1/2 part of Oil of Vitriol, and distill it. Through this, the Oil becomes **fixed** <note>chemically stabilized to resist heat</note>, and the Regulus is multiplied **in power and weight** <note>original: "Virtute & pondere"</note>; once it is prepared in this way, it never needs to be made again.

3. To unite **exalted gold** with **fixed silver** <term>fixed silver</term> <note>original: "☽ fixa"; silver that has been rendered stable against high heat</note>, it is best done **through the wet path** <note>original: "via humida"; chemical reactions performed in liquid solutions rather than by melting solids</note>. One dissolves each in its respective solvent <note>meaning their specific acids</note>, joins them together, and distills the liquid. Then, the required weight of **mercury** <note>represented by the symbol ☿</note> is rubbed into the mixture, evaporated off, burned off with wax, mixed with potash, and projected onto melted gold.

<summary>This page presents three chemical corollaries detailing the use of the Regulus of Venus to refine gold and methods for increasing the potency of alchemical materials using acids and the "wet path."</summary>
<keywords>Corollaries, Regulus of Venus, Gold Exaltation, Calx, Oil of Vitriol, Aqua Fortis, Fixed Silver, Wet Path, Mercury, Potash</keywords>

...and also let it flow well for 2 hours, while observing the proper **manipulations** <note>original: "Handgriffe"; specific manual techniques or steps</note> in every task. If one possesses a proper **Mercurial Water** <term>Mercurial Water</term> <note>a solvent derived from mercury</note> for the **solution** <term>solution</term> <note>the process of dissolving a solid into a liquid</note>, then no **mercury sublimate** <term>mercury sublimate</term> <note>original: "Mercurium sublimatum"; a toxic powder used as a reagent, usually mercuric chloride</note> is required, and this method is the most certain of all.

4. The aforementioned **yellow sulfur** <note>original: "Sulphur"</note> is also better for converting into an **oil** <note>in alchemy, an "oil" refers to a concentrated, liquid essence of a substance</note> than the green variety, and it **tinges** <term>tinges</term> <note>original: "tingiret"; the alchemical process of changing the color or essential nature of a substance</note> more intensely.
